, we apply an argument from
Bandeira et al. 2020

for bounding the
\chi^{2}
-divergence, and track carefully the dependence of
this argument on the dimension
K
.

Lemma 6.2
.

For any
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{2K}
,

D_{\mathrm{KL}}(p_{\theta}\|p_{\theta^{\prime}})\leq\frac{\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}.

(58)

Furthermore, let
\theta=(r_{k}\cos\phi_{k},r_{k}\sin\phi_{k})_{k=1}^{K}

and
\theta^{\prime}=(r_{k}^{\prime}\cos\phi_{k}^{\prime},r_{k}^{\prime}\sin\phi_{k}^{\prime})_{k=1}^{K}
. Denote

R^{2}=\max(\sum_{k=1}^{K}r_{k}^{2}
,
\sum_{k=1}^{K}{r_{k}^{\prime}}^{2})
and

\bar{r}=\max(\max_{k=1}^{K}r_{k},\max_{k=1}^{K}r_{k}^{\prime})
. Then also

\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{KL}}(p_{\theta}\|p_{\theta^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{e^{R^{2}/2\sigma^{2}}}{4\sigma^{4}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}(r_{k}^{2}-{r_{k}^{\prime}}^{2})^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt+\frac{3\bar{r}^{2}R^{2}e^{3R^{2}/2\sigma^{2}}}{2\sigma^{6}}\cdot\inf_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\Big[(r_{k}-r_{k}^{\prime})^{2}+r_{k}r_{k}^{\prime}(\phi_{k}-\phi_{k}^{\prime}+k\alpha)^{2}\Big].

(59)

The upper bound (
59
) is sufficient to prove Lemma

6.1
in the setting
\beta=0
, where the argument is as
follows: We restrict attention to a discrete
space of
2^{K}
parameters
\theta^{\tau}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
, indexed by the hypercube

\tau\in\{0,1\}^{K}
, where all Fourier magnitudes are equal to 1 and
the Fourier phases
\phi^{\tau}=(\phi_{1}^{\tau},\ldots,\phi_{K}^{\tau})
are given by

\phi^{\tau}_{k}=\tau_{k}\cdot\phi.

Here, the value
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
is chosen maximally while ensuring that

D_{\mathrm{KL}}(p_{\theta^{\tau}}\|p_{\theta^{\tau^{\prime}}})\leq H(\tau,\tau^{\prime})/N

by the bounds of Lemma
6.2
, where

H(\tau,\tau^{\prime})
is the Hamming distance on the hypercube. Applying
Proposition
3.1
, we may show that the loss between such
parameters is also lower bounded in terms of Hamming distance as

L(\theta^{\tau},\theta^{\tau^{\prime}})\gtrsim r^{2}\phi^{2}\cdot H(\tau,\tau^{\prime})
. Assouad’s lemma, see e.g.
(
Cai and Zhou 2012
, Lemma
2)
, then implies a minimax lower bound over the discrete
parameter space
\{\theta^{\tau}:\tau\in\{0,1\}^{K}\}
, which in turn implies the
lower bound of Lemma
6.1
over
\mathcal{P}_{0}
.
For more general decay parameters
\beta\in[0,\frac{1}{2})
, we apply
a variation of this argument where the parameters
\theta^{\tau}
are defined such
that only the Fourier phases
\phi_{k}^{\tau}
for
k>K/2
are non-zero. We establish
a modified version of (
59
) for the corresponding
vectors
\theta^{\tau}
, where
\bar{r}
may be replaced by the maximum of

(r_{k},r_{k}^{\prime})
over
k>K/2
. The remainder of the proof is then similar to the

\beta=0
setting.

Appendix A
Proofs for method-of-moments estimation

We prove the results of Section
4
on the method-of-moments estimator.

Proposition A.1
.

Let
\eta\sim\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{C}}(0,2)
. Then we have the equalities in law

\eta\overset{L}{=}\overline{\eta}
and

\eta\overset{L}{=}e^{i\phi}\eta
for any
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
.
Furthermore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\eta^{j}\overline{\eta}^{k}]

\displaystyle=0\text{ for all integers }j\neq k,

(60)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|\eta|^{2j}]

\displaystyle\leq 4^{j}j!\text{ for all integers }j\geq 1.

(61)

Proof.

We may represent
\eta=Re^{i\alpha}
where
R^{2}\sim\chi_{2}^{2}
is independent of

\alpha\sim\operatorname{Unif}([-\pi,\pi))
. Then
\bar{\eta}=Re^{-i\alpha}
,

e^{i\phi}\eta=Re^{i(\phi+\alpha)}
, and
\eta^{j}\bar{\eta}^{k}=R^{j+k}e^{i(j-k)\alpha}
, so
\eta\overset{L}{=}\overline{\eta}
,

\eta\overset{L}{=}e^{i\phi}\eta
, and (
60
) follow.
For (
61
), write
|\eta|^{2}=R^{2}=Z^{2}+{Z^{\prime}}^{2}
where
Z,Z^{\prime}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Then

\mathbb{E}[|\eta|^{2j}]=\mathbb{E}[(Z^{2}+{Z^{\prime}}^{2})^{j}]\leq 2^{j}\mathbb{E}[Z^{2j}+{Z^{\prime}}^{2j}]
.
We have
\mathbb{E}[Z^{2j}]=(2j-1)!!\leq 2^{j-1}\cdot j!
, showing
(
61
).
∎

A.1
Estimation of
r_{k}

Proof of Lemma
4.2
.

Write
\theta_{k}=(\theta_{k,1},\theta_{k,2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and

\varepsilon_{k}^{(m)}=(\varepsilon_{k,1}^{(m)},\varepsilon_{k,2}^{(m)})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Since
|\tilde{y}_{k}^{(m)}|^{2}=\|\theta_{k}+\sigma\varepsilon_{k}^{(m)}\|^{2}

and
\|\theta_{k}\|^{2}=r_{k}^{2}
, we have

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}|\tilde{y}_{k}^{(m)}|^{2}-2\sigma^{2}=r_{k}^{2}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}2\sigma\langle\varepsilon_{k}^{(m)},\theta_{k}\rangle+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}(\|\varepsilon_{k}^{(m)}\|^{2}-2).

Applying
N^{-1}\sum_{m}2\sigma\langle\varepsilon_{k}^{(m)},\theta_{k}\rangle\sim\mathcal{N}(0,4\sigma^{2}r_{k}^{2}/N)
,

\sum_{m}\|\varepsilon_{k}^{(m)}\|^{2}\sim\chi^{2}_{2N}
, and standard Gaussian and
chi-squared tail bounds, for a universal constant
c>0
and any
t>0
we have

\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}2\sigma\langle\varepsilon_{k}^{(m)},\theta_{k}\rangle\geq t\right]\leq e^{-cNt^{2}/\sigma^{2}r_{k}^{2}},\quad\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}(\|\varepsilon_{k}^{(m)}\|^{2}-2)\geq t\right]\leq e^{-cN(t\wedge t^{2})}.

Then for a universal constant
c^{\prime}>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}|\tilde{y}_{k}^{(m)}|^{2}-2\sigma^{2}\geq(1+t)r_{k}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}2\sigma\langle\varepsilon_{k}^{(m)},\theta_{k}\rangle\geq\frac{tr_{k}^{2}}{2}\right]+\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{\sigma^{2}}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}(\|\varepsilon_{k}^{(m)}\|^{2}-2)\geq\frac{tr_{k}^{2}}{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left(-c^{\prime}N\left(\frac{t^{2}r_{k}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\wedge\frac{tr_{k}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\wedge\frac{t^{2}r_{k}^{4}}{\sigma^{4}}\right)\right).

Applying this with
t=2s+s^{2}
and recalling the definition of
\hat{r}_{k}
from
(
16
), the left side is exactly
\mathbb{P}[\hat{r}_{k}\geq r_{k}(1+s)]
.
Then, considering separately the cases
s\geq 1
and
s\leq 1
,
the right side reduces to the upper bound (
23
).
For the lower bound, similarly for any
t>0
,
a lower chi-squared tail bound gives

\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}(\|\varepsilon_{k}^{(m)}\|^{2}-2)\leq-t\right]\leq e^{-cNt^{2}}.

Then we obtain analogously

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}|\tilde{y}_{k}^{(m)}|^{2}-2\sigma^{2}\leq r_{k}^{2}(1-t)\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left(-cN\left(\frac{t^{2}r_{k}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\wedge\frac{t^{2}r_{k}^{4}}{\sigma^{4}}\right)\right).

Applying this with
t=2s-s^{2}\geq s
for
s\in[0,1)
, we obtain
(
24
).
∎

Proof of Corollary
4.3
.

We apply
\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbbm{1}\{|X|\geq s\}\cdot 2s\,ds]=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}[|X|\geq s]\cdot 2s\,ds
with
X=\hat{r}_{k}/r_{k}-1
, and

\int_{0}^{\infty}s\,e^{-\alpha s^{2}}ds=\alpha^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}t\,e^{-t^{2}}dt\leq C/\alpha
. Then Lemma
4.2
gives

\mathbb{E}[(\hat{r}_{k}-r_{k})^{2}]=r_{k}^{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}[(\hat{r}_{k}/r_{k}-1)^{2}]\leq r_{k}^{2}\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}8s\left(e^{-cNs^{2}r_{k}^{2}/\sigma^{2}}+e^{-cNs^{2}r_{k}^{4}/\sigma^{4}}\right)ds\leq C\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{N}+\frac{\sigma^{4}}{Nr_{k}^{2}}\right).

∎

A.2
Oracle estimation of
\Phi_{k,l}

Proof of Lemma
4.4
.

Recall
B_{k,l}
from (
21
) and
\hat{B}_{k,l}
from (
17
).
We first show concentration of
\hat{B}_{k,l}
around
B_{k,l}
. Let us write

\tilde{y}_{k}^{(m)}=r_{k}e^{i(\phi_{k}+k\alpha^{(m)})}+\sigma\tilde{\varepsilon}_{k}^{(m)}=e^{ik\alpha^{(m)}}\tilde{\theta}_{k}(1+(\sigma/r_{k})\eta_{k}^{(m)})

where
\tilde{\theta}_{k}=r_{k}e^{i\phi_{k}}
is the complex representation of

(\theta_{k,1},\theta_{k,2})
,
and
\eta_{k}^{(m)}=e^{-ik\alpha^{(m)}}(r_{k}/\tilde{\theta}_{k})\tilde{\varepsilon}_{k}^{(m)}
is a
rotation of the Gaussian noise. By Proposition
A.1
,
we still have
\eta_{k}^{(m)}\sim\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{C}}(0,2)
where these
remain independent across all
k=1,\ldots,K
and
m=1,\ldots,N
.
Applying this to (
17
),
the factors
e^{ik\alpha^{(m)}},e^{il\alpha^{(m)}},e^{i(k+l)\alpha^{(m)}}

cancel to yield

\hat{B}_{k,l}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\tilde{\theta}_{k+l}\overline{\tilde{\theta}_{k}\tilde{\theta}_{l}}\left(1+(\sigma/r_{k+l})\eta_{k+l}^{(m)}\right)\left(1+(\sigma/r_{k})\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}}\right)\left(1+(\sigma/r_{l})\overline{\eta_{l}^{(m)}}\right)=B_{k,l}(1+\mathrm{I}+\mathrm{II}+\mathrm{III})

(62)

where

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\frac{\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}}}{r_{k}}+\frac{\overline{\eta_{l}^{(m)}}}{r_{l}}+\frac{\eta_{k+l}^{(m)}}{r_{k+l}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{II}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\frac{\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}}\overline{\eta_{l}^{(m)}}}{r_{k}r_{l}}+\frac{\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}}\eta_{k+l}^{(m)}}{r_{k}r_{k+l}}+\frac{\overline{\eta_{l}^{(m)}}\eta_{k+l}^{(m)}}{r_{l}r_{k+l}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{III}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma^{3}}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\frac{\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}}\overline{\eta_{l}^{(m)}}\eta_{k+l}^{(m)}}{r_{k}r_{l}r_{k+l}}.

To bound
\mathrm{I}
, observe that

\frac{\sigma}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\frac{\operatorname{Re}\eta_{k}^{(m)}}{r_{k}}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\frac{\sigma^{2}}{Nr_{k}^{2}}\right),

(63)

and similarly for the imaginary part and for the other two terms of
\mathrm{I}
. Then by a Gaussian tail bound,

\mathbb{P}[|\mathrm{I}|\geq t]\leq C\exp(-cNt^{2}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}/\sigma^{2}).

(64)

To bound
\mathrm{II}
, consider first
k\neq l
and

\sum_{m}\operatorname{Re}\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}}\cdot\operatorname{Re}\overline{\eta_{l}^{(m)}}
.
Each term
\operatorname{Re}\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}}\cdot\operatorname{Re}\overline{\eta_{l}^{(m)}}
is the product of two independent
standard Gaussian variables.
Then applying
(
Latala 2006
, Corollary 1)
with
d=2
,
A=I
,

\|A\|_{\{1,2\}}=\sqrt{N}
, and
\|A\|_{\{1\},\{2\}}=1
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\operatorname{Re}\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}}\cdot\operatorname{Re}\overline{\eta_{l}^{(m)}}\right|\geq t\right]\leq Ce^{-cN(t\wedge t^{2})}.

So

\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\frac{\sigma^{2}}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\frac{\operatorname{Re}\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}}}{r_{k}}\cdot\frac{\operatorname{Re}\overline{\eta_{l}^{(m)}}}{r_{l}}\right|\geq t\right]\leq C\exp\left(-cN\left(\frac{t\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\wedge\frac{t^{2}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{4}}{\sigma^{4}}\right)\right).

(65)

The same bound holds for all products of real and imaginary parts of

\eta_{k}^{(m)}
and
\eta_{l}^{(m)}
, except for

\operatorname{Re}\eta_{k}^{(m)}\cdot\operatorname{Re}\eta_{l}^{(m)}
and

\operatorname{Im}\eta_{k}^{(m)}\cdot\operatorname{Im}\eta_{l}^{(m)}
when
k=l
. For these products, we may
consider them together and apply

\frac{\sigma^{2}}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\frac{(\operatorname{Re}\eta_{k}^{(m)})^{2}}{r_{k}^{2}}-\frac{(\operatorname{Im}\eta_{k}^{(m)})^{2}}{r_{k}^{2}}=\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{Nr_{k}^{2}}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\frac{\operatorname{Re}\eta_{k}^{(m)}-\operatorname{Im}\eta_{k}^{(m)}}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot\frac{\operatorname{Re}\eta_{k}^{(m)}+\operatorname{Im}\eta_{k}^{(m)}}{\sqrt{2}}

where now
(\operatorname{Re}\eta_{k}^{(m)}-\operatorname{Im}\eta_{k}^{(m)})/\sqrt{2}
and

(\operatorname{Re}\eta_{k}^{(m)}+\operatorname{Im}\eta_{k}^{(m)})/\sqrt{2}
are independent standard Gaussian
variables. The bound (
65
) then holds for this sum, and this
shows

\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\frac{\sigma^{2}}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\frac{\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}}\overline{\eta_{l}^{(m)}}}{r_{k}r_{l}}\right|>t\right]\leq C\exp\left(-cN\left(\frac{t\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\wedge\frac{t^{2}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{4}}{\sigma^{4}}\right)\right)

for the first term of
\mathrm{II}
.
Applying the same argument for the remaining two terms of
\mathrm{II}
,

\mathbb{P}[|\mathrm{II}|\geq t]\leq C\exp\left(-cN\left(\frac{t\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\wedge\frac{t^{2}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{4}}{\sigma^{4}}\right)\right).

(66)

We apply a similar argument to bound
\mathrm{III}
.
Consider first
k\neq l
and

\sum_{m}\operatorname{Re}\eta_{k}^{(m)}\cdot\operatorname{Re}\eta_{l}^{(m)}\cdot\operatorname{Re}\eta_{k+l}^{(m)}
.
Each term
\operatorname{Re}\eta_{k}^{(m)}\cdot\operatorname{Re}\eta_{l}^{(m)}\cdot\operatorname{Re}\eta_{k+l}^{(m)}

is the product of three independent standard Gaussian variables. Then
applying
(
Latala 2006
, Corollary 1)
with
d=3
,
A=\sum_{m=1}^{N}e_{m}\otimes e_{m}\otimes e_{m}
,
\|A\|_{\{1,2,3\}}=\sqrt{N}
,

\|A\|_{\{1,2\},\{3\}}=1
, and
\|A\|_{\{1\},\{2\},\{3\}}=1
,

\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\operatorname{Re}\eta_{k}^{(m)}\cdot\operatorname{Re}\eta_{l}^{(m)}\cdot\operatorname{Re}\eta_{k+l}^{(m)}\right|\geq t\right]\leq Ce^{-c(Nt^{2}\wedge Nt\wedge(Nt)^{2/3})}\leq Ce^{-cN(t^{2}\wedge\frac{t^{2/3}}{N^{1/3}})}.

(The second inequality applies
t\geq t^{2}\wedge\frac{t^{2/3}}{N^{1/3}}
for any
N\geq 1
and
t\geq 0
.)
The same bound holds for all combinations of real and imaginary parts of

\eta_{k}^{(m)},\eta_{l}^{(m)},\eta_{k+l}^{(m)}
, except again for products having

\operatorname{Re}\eta_{k}^{(m)}\cdot\operatorname{Re}\eta_{l}^{(m)}
or

\operatorname{Im}\eta_{k}^{(m)}\cdot\operatorname{Im}\eta_{l}^{(m)}
when
k=l
. These products may be
bounded by applying

\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Re}\eta_{2k}^{(m)}\cdot\left((\operatorname{Re}\eta_{k}^{(m)})^{2}-(\operatorname{Im}\eta_{k}^{(m)})^{2}\right)=\operatorname{Re}\eta_{2k}^{(m)}\cdot\frac{\operatorname{Re}\eta_{k}^{(m)}-\operatorname{Im}\eta_{k}^{(m)}}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot\frac{\operatorname{Re}\eta_{k}^{(m)}+\operatorname{Im}\eta_{k}^{(m)}}{\sqrt{2}}

and similarly for
\operatorname{Im}\eta_{2k}^{(m)}\cdot((\operatorname{Re}\eta_{k}^{(m)})^{2}-(\operatorname{Im}\eta_{k}^{(m)})^{2})
, where
\operatorname{Re}\eta_{2k}^{(m)}
,

\operatorname{Im}\eta_{2k}^{(m)}
,
(\operatorname{Re}\eta_{k}^{(m)}-\operatorname{Im}\eta_{k}^{(m)})/\sqrt{2}
, and

(\operatorname{Re}\eta_{k}^{(m)}+\operatorname{Im}\eta_{k}^{(m)})/\sqrt{2}
are independent standard Gaussian
variables. Thus

\mathbb{P}\left[|\mathrm{III}|\geq t\right]\leq C\exp\left(-cN\left(\frac{t^{2}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{6}}{\sigma^{6}}\wedge\frac{t^{2/3}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}{N^{1/3}\sigma^{2}}\right)\right).

(67)

Combining (
64
), (
66
), and
(
67
), for any
s>0
we obtain

\mathbb{P}\Big[|\hat{B}_{k,l}/B_{k,l}-1|\geq s\Big]\leq C\exp\left(-cN\left(\frac{s^{2}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\wedge\frac{s\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\wedge\frac{s^{2}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{4}}{\sigma^{4}}\wedge\frac{s^{2}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{6}}{\sigma^{6}}\wedge\frac{s^{2/3}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}{N^{1/3}\sigma^{2}}\right)\right).

We have

\frac{s\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\geq\frac{s^{2}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\wedge\frac{s^{2/3}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}{N^{1/3}\sigma^{2}},\qquad\frac{s^{2}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{4}}{\sigma^{4}}\geq\frac{s^{2}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\wedge\frac{s^{2}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{6}}{\sigma^{6}},

so this simplifies to (
25
).

Finally, for any
z\in\mathbb{C}
and any
s\in(0,1)
, observe
that
|z-1|<s
implies
|\operatorname{Arg}z|<\arcsin s<\pi s/2
for the principal
argument (
11
). Then, recalling that

\hat{\Phi}_{k,l}^{\mathrm{oracle}}-\Phi_{k,l}=\operatorname{Arg}(\hat{B}_{k,l}/B_{k,l})
from
(
22
), we obtain for any
s\in(0,\pi/2)
that

\mathbb{P}\Big[|\hat{\Phi}_{k,l}^{\mathrm{oracle}}-\Phi_{k,l}|\geq s\Big]\leq\mathbb{P}\Big[|\hat{B}_{k,l}/B_{k,l}-1|\geq\frac{2s}{\pi}\Big]

and (
26
) follows.
∎

Proof of Corollary
4.5
.

We apply
\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}[|X|\geq s]\cdot 2s\,ds

and Lemma
4.4
to obtain, for universal constants
C,c>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\hat{\Phi}_{k,l}^{\mathrm{oracle}}-\Phi_{k,l})^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\pi/2}Cs\left(e^{-cNs^{2}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}/\sigma^{2}}+e^{-cNs^{2}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{6}/\sigma^{6}}+e^{-c(Ns)^{2/3}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}/\sigma^{2}}\right)ds

\displaystyle\hskip 144.54pt+C\left(e^{-cN\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}/\sigma^{2}}+e^{-cN\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{6}/\sigma^{6}}+e^{-cN^{2/3}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}/\sigma^{2}}\right),

where the second term bounds the integral from
s=\pi/2
to
s=\pi
.
The result then follows from applying

\int_{0}^{\infty}se^{-\alpha s^{2}}ds=\alpha^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}te^{-t^{2}}dt\leq C/\alpha
and
\int_{0}^{\infty}se^{-\alpha s^{2/3}}ds=\alpha^{-3}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{3}e^{-t^{2}}\cdot 3t^{2}\,dt\leq C/\alpha^{3}
for the first term,

e^{-cx}\leq C/x
and
e^{-cx}\leq C/x^{3}
for the second term, and

\sigma^{6}/N^{2}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{6}\leq\sigma^{6}/N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{6}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.6
.

Part (c) follows from Corollary
4.5
and Cauchy-Schwarz. For part
(a), recall from (
22
) and the expression for
\hat{B}_{k,l}
in
(
62
) that

\hat{\Phi}_{k,l}^{\mathrm{oracle}}-\Phi_{k,l}=\operatorname{Arg}(\hat{B}_{k,l}/B_{k,l})=\operatorname{Arg}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\left(1+(\sigma/r_{k+l})\eta_{k+l}^{(m)}\right)\left(1+(\sigma/r_{k})\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}}\right)\left(1+(\sigma/r_{l})\overline{\eta_{l}^{(m)}}\right)

Since
\eta_{k}^{(m)}
are independent across
k=1,\ldots,K
and
m=1,\ldots,N
,
we obtain in the setting of part (a) that
\hat{\Phi}_{k,l}^{\mathrm{oracle}}-\Phi_{k,l}
is
independent of
\hat{\Phi}_{x,y}^{\mathrm{oracle}}-\Phi_{x,y}
.
Furthermore, applying the conjugation symmetry of
Proposition
A.1
to the variables
\eta_{k}^{(m)}
,
we have the equality in law

\hat{B}_{k,l}/B_{k,l}\overset{L}{=}\overline{\hat{B}_{k,l}/B_{k,l}}
for the
quantity inside
\operatorname{Arg}(\cdot)
. Since
\operatorname{Arg}z=-\operatorname{Arg}\overline{z}
whenever

\operatorname{Arg}z\neq-\pi
, and the probability is 0 that

\operatorname{Arg}\hat{B}_{k,l}/B_{k,l}=-\pi
exactly, this equality in law implies
the sign symmetry (
27
). Hence

\mathbb{E}[\hat{\Phi}_{k,l}^{\mathrm{oracle}}-\Phi_{k,l}]=0
. This shows part (a).

It remains to show part (b). Let
\operatorname{Ln}
denote the principal value of the
complex logarithm with branch cut on the negative real line, so that

\operatorname{Arg}z=\operatorname{Im}\operatorname{Ln}z
whenever
\operatorname{Arg}z\neq-\pi
. Denote

\delta_{k,l}=\hat{B}_{k,l}/B_{k,l}-1
. Then (with probability 1)

\displaystyle(\hat{\Phi}_{k,l}^{\mathrm{oracle}}-\Phi_{k,l})(\hat{\Phi}_{x,y}^{\mathrm{oracle}}-\Phi_{x,y})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Arg}\frac{\hat{B}_{k,l}}{B_{k,l}}\cdot\operatorname{Arg}\frac{\hat{B}_{x,y}}{B_{x,y}}=\operatorname{Im}\operatorname{Ln}\left(1+\delta_{k,l}\right)\cdot\operatorname{Im}\operatorname{Ln}\left(1+\delta_{x,y}\right).

Let us fix an integer
J=J(N,\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2},\sigma^{2})\geq 1
to be determined, and
apply a Taylor expansion of
t\mapsto\operatorname{Ln}(1+t\delta)
around
t=0
to write

\operatorname{Im}\operatorname{Ln}(1+\delta)=q(\delta)+r(\delta),\qquad q(\delta)=\operatorname{Im}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\frac{(-1)^{j-1}}{j}\delta^{j},\qquad r(\delta)=\operatorname{Im}\int_{0}^{1}\delta^{J+1}\cdot\frac{(-1)^{J}(1-t)^{J}}{(1+t\delta)^{J+1}}dt.

Define the event

\mathcal{E}=\{|\delta_{k,l}|<1/2\text{ and }|\delta_{x,y}|<1/2\}
. We may then
apply the approximation

\mathbb{E}\left[(\hat{\Phi}_{k,l}^{\mathrm{oracle}}-\Phi_{k,l})(\hat{\Phi}_{x,y}^{\mathrm{oracle}}-\Phi_{x,y})\right]=\mathbb{E}\Big[q(\delta_{k,l})\cdot q(\delta_{x,y})\Big]+\mathrm{I}+\mathrm{II}+\mathrm{III}

where we define the three error terms

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{E}^{c}\}\cdot q(\delta_{k,l})\cdot q(\delta_{x,y})\Big]

\displaystyle\mathrm{II}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{E}\}\Big(q(\delta_{k,l})\cdot r(\delta_{x,y})+r(\delta_{k,l})\cdot q(\delta_{x,y})+r(\delta_{k,l})\cdot r(\delta_{x,y})\Big)\right]

\displaystyle\mathrm{III}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{E}^{c}\}\cdot(\hat{\Phi}_{k,l}^{\mathrm{oracle}}-\Phi_{k,l})(\hat{\Phi}_{x,y}^{\mathrm{oracle}}-\Phi_{x,y})\right]

To bound these errors, let
C,C^{\prime},c,c^{\prime}>0
denote
universal constants changing from instance to instance. Recall
from (
25
) that

\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}^{c}]\leq\mathbb{P}[|\delta_{k,l}|\geq 1/2]+\mathbb{P}[|\delta_{x,y}|\geq 1/2]\leq Ce^{-c(\frac{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{6}}{\sigma^{6}}\wedge\frac{N^{2/3}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}})}.

(68)

Also, for any
j\geq 1
,
applying
\mathbb{E}[|X|^{j}]=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}[|X|\geq s]\cdot js^{j-1}\,ds

with
X=2\delta_{k,l}
, and applying also for
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
that

\mathbb{E}[|Z|^{j}]\leq\mathbb{E}[Z^{2j}]^{1/2}=[(2j-1)!!]^{1/2}\leq(2j)^{(j-1)/2}
, we have from (
25
) that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|2\delta_{k,l}|^{j}]

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}js^{j-1}\cdot C(e^{-cNs^{2}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}/\sigma^{2}}+e^{-cNs^{2}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{6}/\sigma^{6}}+e^{-c(Ns)^{2/3}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}/\sigma^{2}})\,ds

\displaystyle=Cj\left(\left(\frac{\sigma^{j}}{\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{j}N^{j/2}}+\frac{\sigma^{3j}}{\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{3j}N^{j/2}}\right)\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{j-1}e^{-ct^{2}}dt+\left(\frac{\sigma^{3j}}{\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{3j}N^{j}}\right)\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{3j-3}e^{-ct^{2}}\cdot 3t^{2}\,dt\right)

\displaystyle\leq(C_{0}j)^{\frac{j}{2}}\left(\frac{\sigma^{j}}{\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{j}N^{j/2}}+\frac{\sigma^{3j}}{\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{3j}N^{j/2}}\right)+(C_{0}j)^{\frac{3j}{2}}\left(\frac{\sigma^{3j}}{\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{3j}N^{j}}\right)

where
C_{0}
in the last line is a universal constant, which we will later
assume satisfies
C_{0}\geq 3
. Let us set

J=\left\lfloor\frac{1}{4C_{0}e}\left(\frac{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{6}}{\sigma^{6}}\wedge\frac{N^{2/3}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)\right\rfloor

(69)

for this constant
C_{0}>0
. Note that if the quantity inside
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
is less than 1, then the statement of part (b) holds since the left
side of (
28
) is at most
\pi^{2}
, and the right side is an
arbitrarily large constant. Thus, we may assume henceforth that
J\geq 1
.
The above gives

\mathbb{E}[|2\delta_{k,l}|^{j}]\leq 2\left(\frac{j}{4eJ}\right)^{j/2}+\left(\frac{j}{4eJ}\right)^{3j/2}.

Applying
|q(\delta)|\leq J\max(|\delta|,|\delta|^{J})
, and also

|r(\delta)|\leq|2\delta|^{J+1}
for
|\delta|<1/2
, this shows

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|q(\delta_{k,l})|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq J^{2}\mathbb{E}[|\delta_{k,l}|^{2}]+J^{2}\mathbb{E}[|\delta|^{2J}]\leq CJ+J^{2}e^{-cJ},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|q(\delta_{k,l})|^{3}]

\displaystyle\leq J^{3}\mathbb{E}[|\delta_{k,l}|^{3}]+J^{3}\mathbb{E}[|\delta|^{3J}]\leq CJ^{3/2}+J^{3}e^{-cJ},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{E}\}|r(\delta_{k,l})|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}[|\delta_{k,l}|^{2J+2}]\leq Ce^{-cJ}.

Then, applying these bounds together with (
68
),
Hölder’s inequality, and Cauchy-Schwarz,

|\mathrm{I}|+|\mathrm{II}|+|\mathrm{III}|\leq CJ^{2}e^{-cJ}\leq C^{\prime}e^{-c^{\prime}J}.

This gives the second term on the right side of (
28
).

Finally, let us bound the dominant term
\mathbb{E}[q(\delta_{k,l})q(\delta_{x,y})]

using the condition that
\{k,l,k+l\}\cap\{x,y,x+y\}
has cardinality 1. Applying
2\operatorname{Im}u\cdot\operatorname{Im}v=\operatorname{Re}u\bar{v}-\operatorname{Re}uv
, we have

\mathbb{E}[q(\delta_{k,l})q(\delta_{x,y})]=\sum_{i,j=1}^{J}\frac{(-1)^{i+j}}{ij}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Im}\delta_{k,l}^{i}\cdot\operatorname{Im}\delta_{x,y}^{j}]=\sum_{i,j=1}^{J}\frac{(-1)^{i+j}}{2ij}\Big(\operatorname{Re}\mathbb{E}[\delta_{k,l}^{i}\overline{\delta_{x,y}^{j}}]-\operatorname{Re}\mathbb{E}[\delta_{k,l}^{i}\delta_{x,y}^{j}]\Big).

From the expression for
\hat{B}_{k,l}
in (
62
), observe
that

\delta_{k,l}=\frac{\hat{B}_{k,l}}{B_{k,l}}-1=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\left(1+\frac{\sigma}{r_{k+l}}\eta_{k+l}^{(m)}\right)\left(1+\frac{\sigma}{r_{k}}\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}}\right)\left(1+\frac{\sigma}{r_{l}}\overline{\eta_{l}^{(m)}}\right)-1.

We view this as a polynomial in the variables

\{\eta_{k+l}^{(m)},\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}},\overline{\eta_{l}^{(m)}}:m=1,\ldots,N\}
where,
after canceling
+1
with
-1
, each monomial has total degree at least 1
in these variables. We consider three cases.

Case 1:

k+l=x+y
. This allows possibly
k=l
and/or
x=y
,
but ensures
\{k,l\}\cap\{x,y\}=\emptyset
since
\{k,l,k+l\}\cap\{x,y,x+y\}
has cardinality 1. We may
expand
\delta_{k,l}^{i}\delta_{x,y}^{j}
as
a sum of monomials in
\eta_{k+l}^{(m)},\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}},\overline{\eta_{l}^{(m)}},\overline{\eta_{x}^{(m)}},\overline{\eta_{y}^{(m)}}
with
degree at least 1, and observe that
k+l=x+y
is distinct from
\{k,l,x,y\}

because it is strictly greater in value.
Then (
60
) from Proposition

A.1
implies
\mathbb{E}[\delta_{k,l}^{i}\delta_{x,y}^{j}]=0
.
We may also expand
\delta_{k,l}^{i}\overline{\delta_{x,y}^{j}}
as a sum of
monomials in
\eta_{k+l}^{(m)},\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}},\overline{\eta_{l}^{(m)}},\overline{\eta_{k+l}^{(m)}},\eta_{x}^{(m)},\eta_{y}^{(m)}
. Since
\{k,l\}
are distinct from
\{x,y,k+l\}
, any monomial
involving
\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}},\overline{\eta_{l}^{(m)}}
has vanishing
expectation. Similarly, any monomial involving

\eta_{x}^{(m)},\eta_{y}^{(m)}
has vanishing expectation. Thus
the only non-vanishing terms are

\mathbb{E}[\delta_{k,l}^{i}\overline{\delta_{x,y}^{j}}]=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\frac{\sigma}{r_{k+l}}\eta_{k+l}^{(m)}\right)^{i}\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\frac{\sigma}{r_{k+l}}\overline{\eta_{k+l}^{(m)}}\right)^{j}\right].

(70)

Then applying the equality in law

N^{-1}\sum_{m=1}^{N}(\sigma/r_{k+l})\eta_{k+l}^{(m)}\overset{L}{=}\eta\cdot\sigma/(r_{k+l}\sqrt{N})
where
\eta\sim\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{C}}(0,2)
,
together with (
60
) and (
61
)
and the bound
j!\leq j^{j}
,

\Big|\mathbb{E}[\delta_{k,l}^{i}\overline{\delta_{x,y}^{j}}]\Big|=\left(\frac{\sigma}{r_{k+l}\sqrt{N}}\right)^{i+j}\mathbb{E}[\eta^{i}\overline{\eta^{j}}]\leq\mathbbm{1}\{i=j\}\left(\frac{4j\sigma^{2}}{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}\right)^{j}.

So, recalling the definition of
J
from (
69
) where
C_{0}\geq 3
,

\Big|\mathbb{E}[q(\delta_{k,l})q(\delta_{x,y})]\Big|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{J}\frac{1}{2j^{2}}\left(\frac{4j\sigma^{2}}{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}\right)^{j}\leq\left(\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}\right)\sum_{j=1}^{J}\left(\frac{4J\sigma^{2}}{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}\right)^{j-1}\leq\left(\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}\right)\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{C_{0}e}\right)^{j-1}.

Thus we obtain, for a universal constant
C>0
,

\Big|\mathbb{E}[q(\delta_{k,l})q(\delta_{x,y})]\Big|\leq\frac{C\sigma^{2}}{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}.

(71)

This concludes the proof in Case 1.

Case 2:

k=x+y
. (By symmetry, this addresses also
l=x+y
,

x=k+l
, and
y=k+l
.) Then
\{k,l\}\cap\{x,y,k+l\}=\emptyset

and
\{k+l\}\cap\{x,y\}=\emptyset
, because
k+l
is greater than
\{k,l\}
,

k
is greater than
\{x,y\}
, and
l=x
or
l=y

would imply that
\{k,l,k+l\}\cap\{x,y,x+y\}
has cardinality 2.
We may expand
\delta_{k,l}^{i}\overline{\delta_{x,y}^{j}}

as a sum of monomials in
\eta_{k+l}^{(m)},\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}},\overline{\eta_{l}^{(m)}},\eta_{x}^{(m)},\eta_{y}^{(m)}
. Since
\{k,l\}
are distinct
from
\{x,y,k+l\}
, (
60
) implies

\mathbb{E}[\delta_{k,l}^{i}\overline{\delta_{x,y}^{j}}]=0
. We may also expand

\delta_{k,l}^{i}\delta_{x,y}^{j}
as a sum of monomials in

\eta_{k+l}^{(m)},\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}},\overline{\eta_{l}^{(m)}},\eta_{k}^{(m)},\overline{\eta_{x}^{(m)}},\overline{\eta_{y}^{(m)}}
. Here,
k+l
is
distinct from
\{k,l,x,y\}
, and
\{x,y\}
are distinct from
\{k,k+l\}
,
so any monomial involving

\eta_{k+l}^{(m)},\overline{\eta_{x}^{(m)}},\overline{\eta_{y}^{(m)}}
has vanishing
expectation. If
l\neq k
, then also
l
is distinct from
\{k,k+l\}
so
monomials involving
\overline{\eta_{l}^{(m)}}
have vanishing expectation,
yielding

\mathbb{E}[\delta_{k,l}^{i}\delta_{x,y}^{j}]=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\frac{\sigma}{r_{k}}\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}}\right)^{i}\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\frac{\sigma}{r_{k}}\eta_{k}^{(m)}\right)^{j}\right].

This is analogous to (
70
), and the same argument as
above gives (
71
).

If instead,
l=k
, then we obtain that the only non-zero terms of

\mathbb{E}[\delta_{k,l}^{i}\delta_{x,y}^{j}]
are

\mathbb{E}\left[\delta_{k,l}^{i}\delta_{x,y}^{j}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\frac{2\sigma}{r_{k}}\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}}+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{r_{k}^{2}}(\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}})^{2}\right)^{i}\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\frac{\sigma}{r_{k}}\eta_{k}^{(m)}\right)^{j}\right]

(72)

Let us distribute this product and then factor the expectations of the
resulting terms, using independence of

\{\eta_{k}^{(m)}:m=1,\ldots,N\}
.
We write
\sum_{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})|a}
for the sum over all
tuples of nonnegative integers
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})
that sum to
a
. Then the
above may be rewritten as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\delta_{k,l}^{i}\delta_{x,y}^{j}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\sum_{a,b\geq 0}}_{a+b=i}\binom{i}{a}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\frac{2\sigma}{r_{k}}\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}}\right)^{a}\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{r_{k}^{2}}(\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}})^{2}\right)^{b}\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\frac{\sigma}{r_{k}}\eta_{k}^{(m)}\right)^{j}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\sum_{a,b\geq 0}}_{a+b=i}\binom{i}{a}2^{a}N^{b}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{m=1}^{N}\frac{\sigma}{Nr_{k}}\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}}\right)^{a}\left(\sum_{m=1}^{N}\left(\frac{\sigma}{Nr_{k}}\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}}\right)^{2}\right)^{b}\left(\sum_{m=1}^{N}\frac{\sigma}{Nr_{k}}\eta_{k}^{(m)}\right)^{j}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\sum_{a,b\geq 0}}_{a+b=i}\binom{i}{a}2^{a}N^{b}\sum_{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})|a}\,\sum_{(b_{1},\ldots,b_{N})|b}\,\sum_{(j_{1},\ldots,j_{N})|j}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt\binom{a}{a_{1},\ldots,a_{N}}\binom{b}{b_{1},\ldots,b_{N}}\binom{j}{j_{1},\ldots,j_{N}}\prod_{m=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{\sigma\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}}}{Nr_{k}}\right)^{a_{m}+2b_{m}}\left(\frac{\sigma\eta_{k}^{(m)}}{Nr_{k}}\right)^{j_{m}}\right]

where the last line uses that there are
\binom{a}{a_{1},\ldots,a_{N}}
ways to
choose
a_{1}
of the factors
(\sum_{m=1}^{N}\frac{\sigma}{Nr_{k}}\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}})^{a}
to correspond to
m=1
,
a_{2}
to
correspond to
m=2
, etc., and similarly for
b
and
j
.

Then, applying (
60
) and (
61
),

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\delta_{k,l}^{i}\delta_{x,y}^{j}\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mathop{\sum_{a,b\geq 0}}_{a+b=i}\binom{i}{a}2^{a}N^{b}\sum_{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})|a}\,\sum_{(b_{1},\ldots,b_{N})|b}\,\sum_{(j_{1},\ldots,j_{N})|j}

\displaystyle\hskip 36.135pt\binom{a}{a_{1},\ldots,a_{N}}\binom{b}{b_{1},\ldots,b_{N}}\binom{j}{j_{1},\ldots,j_{N}}\prod_{m=1}^{N}\mathbbm{1}\{a_{m}+2b_{m}=j_{m}\}\left(\frac{4\sigma^{2}}{N^{2}r_{k}^{2}}\right)^{j_{m}}j_{m}!

Observe that
\binom{j}{j_{1},\ldots,j_{N}}\cdot\prod_{m=1}^{N}j_{m}!=j!\leq j^{j}
.
The condition
a_{m}+2b_{m}=j_{m}
for every
m=1,\ldots,N
requires

a+2b=\sum_{m}a_{m}+2b_{m}=\sum_{m}j_{m}=j
. For
(a,b,j)
satisfying this requirement,
fixing any partitions
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})|a
and
(b_{1},\ldots,b_{N})|b
, there is
exactly one partition
(j_{1},\ldots,j_{N})|j
for which
a_{m}+2b_{m}=j_{m}
holds
for every
m=1,\ldots,N
. Thus, the above gives

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\delta_{k,l}^{i}\delta_{x,y}^{j}\right]\right|\leq\left(\frac{4j\sigma^{2}}{N^{2}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}\right)^{j}\mathop{\sum_{a,b\geq 0}}_{a+b=i,\,a+2b=j}\binom{i}{a}2^{a}N^{b}\sum_{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})|a}\,\sum_{(b_{1},\ldots,b_{N})|b}\,\binom{a}{a_{1},\ldots,a_{N}}\binom{b}{b_{1},\ldots,b_{N}}.

Observe now that
\sum_{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})|a}\binom{a}{a_{1},\ldots,a_{N}}
counts
exactly the number of assignments of each of
a
labeled objects to
N
bins,
by first determining the number of objects in each bin, followed by their
identities. So
\sum_{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{N})|a}\binom{a}{a_{1},\ldots,a_{N}}=N^{a}
.
Applying the similar identity for
b
and
N^{a+2b}=N^{j}
above,

\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\delta_{k,l}^{i}\delta_{x,y}^{j}\right]\right|\leq\left(\frac{4j\sigma^{2}}{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}\right)^{j}\mathop{\sum_{a,b\geq 0}}_{a+b=i,\,a+2b=j}\binom{i}{a}2^{a}.

Then, recalling that
\mathbb{E}\left[\delta_{k,l}^{i}\overline{\delta_{x,y}^{j}}\right]=0
,

\Big|\mathbb{E}[q(\delta_{k,l})q(\delta_{x,y})]\Big|\leq\sum_{i,j=1}^{J}\frac{1}{2ij}\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\delta_{k,l}^{i}\delta_{x,y}^{j}\right]\right|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{J}\frac{1}{2j}\left(\frac{4j\sigma^{2}}{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}\right)^{j}\mathop{\sum_{a,b\geq 0}}_{a+2b=j}\frac{1}{a+b}\binom{a+b}{a}2^{a}.

We may apply

\mathop{\sum_{a,b\geq 0}}_{a+2b=j}\frac{1}{a+b}\binom{a+b}{a}2^{a}\leq\sum_{a=0}^{j}\binom{j}{a}2^{a}=3^{j}.

Then, recalling the definition of
J
from (
69
) where

C_{0}\geq 3
,

\Big|\mathbb{E}[q(\delta_{k,l})q(\delta_{x,y})]\Big|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{J}\frac{1}{2j}\left(\frac{12j\sigma^{2}}{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}\right)^{j}\leq\frac{6\sigma^{2}}{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\left(\frac{12J\sigma^{2}}{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}\right)^{j-1}\leq\frac{6\sigma^{2}}{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{3}{C_{0}e}\right)^{j-1}.

This again yields (
71
), and concludes the proof in Case 2.

Case 3:

k=x
. (By symmetry, this addresses also
k=y
,
l=x
, and
l=y
.)
This ensures that
\{k+l,k+y\}\cap\{k,l,y\}=\emptyset
and
k+l\neq k+y
,
because
k+l
is greater than
\{k,l\}
,
k+y
is greater than
\{k,y\}
,
and
k+l=y
or
k+y=l
or
k+l=k+y

would lead to
\{k,l,k+l\}\cap\{x,y,x+y\}
having cardinality 2.
We may expand
\delta_{k,l}^{i}\delta_{x,y}^{j}
as monomials in

\eta_{k+l}^{(m)},\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}},\overline{\eta_{l}^{(m)}},\eta_{k+y}^{(m)},\overline{\eta_{y}^{(m)}}
. Since
\{k+l,k+y\}
are distinct from

\{k,l,y\}
, (
60
) implies

\mathbb{E}[\delta_{k,l}^{i}\delta_{x,y}^{j}]=0
. We may also expand

\delta_{k,l}^{i}\overline{\delta_{x,y}^{j}}
as monomials in

\eta_{k+l}^{(m)},\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}},\overline{\eta_{l}^{(m)}},\overline{\eta_{k+y}^{(m)}},\eta_{k}^{(m)},\eta_{y}^{(m)}
. Since
k+l
is distinct
from
\{k,l,k+y\}
and
k+y
is distinct from
\{k,y,k+l\}
,
(
60
) implies that any monomials
involving
\eta_{k+l}^{(m)}
or
\overline{\eta_{x+y}^{(m)}}
have vanishing
expectation. Note that since
k+l\neq k+y
, also
l\neq y
.
If
k
is distinct from
\{l,y\}
, then
monomials involving
\overline{\eta_{l}^{(m)}}
or
\eta_{y}^{(m)}
also have
vanishing expectation, so

\mathbb{E}[\delta_{k,l}^{i}\overline{\delta_{x,y}^{j}}]=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\frac{\sigma}{r_{k}}\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}}\right)^{i}\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\frac{\sigma}{r_{k}}\eta_{k}^{(m)}\right)^{j}\right].

This is analogous to (
70
), and the same argument as in
Case 1 leads to (
71
). If instead
k=l
(which by symmetry
addresses also
k=y
), then

\mathbb{E}[\delta_{k,l}^{i}\overline{\delta_{x,y}^{j}}]=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\frac{2\sigma}{r_{k}}\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}}+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{r_{k}^{2}}(\overline{\eta_{k}^{(m)}})^{2}\right)^{i}\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\frac{\sigma}{r_{k}}\eta_{k}^{(m)}\right)^{j}\right].

This is analogous to (
72
), and the same argument as in
Case 2 leads to (
71
). This concludes the proof in Case 3.
Combining these three cases shows (
28
).
∎

A.3
Oracle estimation of
\phi_{k}

Proof of Lemma
4.7
.

Suppose
M\phi=0
. Then for all
(k,l)\in\mathcal{I}
,

\phi_{k+l}=\phi_{k}+\phi_{l}
.
Then
\phi_{2}=\phi_{1}+\phi_{1}=2\phi_{1}
,
\phi_{3}=\phi_{2}+\phi_{1}=3\phi_{1}
, etc., and

\phi_{K}=\phi_{K-1}+\phi_{1}=K\phi_{1}
, so
\phi
is a multiple of

(1,2,3,\ldots,K)
. Conversely, any multiple of
(1,2,3,\ldots,K)
satisfies

M\phi=0
by the definition of
M
. This shows the first statement.

Denote
T=M^{\top}M
. We explicitly compute
T
:
Let
M_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{I}}
be the
k^{\text{th}}
column of
M
. The diagonal entries of
T
are

T_{kk}=\|M_{k}\|^{2}
. If
2k>K
, then the non-zero entries of
M_{k}

correspond to the
(i,j)
pairs

\displaystyle(i,j)=(1,k-1),\ldots,(k-1,1)

\displaystyle:M_{(i,j),k}=1

\displaystyle(i,j)=(1,k),\ldots,(K-k,k)

\displaystyle:M_{(i,j),k}=-1

\displaystyle(i,j)=(k,1),\ldots,(k,K-k)

\displaystyle:M_{(i,j),k}=-1

So
T_{kk}=(k-1)+2(K-k)=2K-1-k
. If
2k\leq K
, then the non-zero entries of

M_{k}
correspond to the
(i,j)
pairs

\displaystyle(i,j)=(1,k-1),\ldots,(k-1,1)

\displaystyle:M_{(i,j),k}=1

\displaystyle(i,j)=(1,k),\ldots,(k-1,k),(k+1,k),\ldots,(K-k,k)

\displaystyle:M_{(i,j),k}=-1

\displaystyle(i,j)=(k,1),\ldots,(k,k-1),(k,k+1),\ldots,(k,K-k)

\displaystyle:M_{(i,j),k}=-1

\displaystyle(i,j)=(k,k)

\displaystyle:M_{(i,j),k}=-2

So
T_{kk}=(k-1)+2(K-k-1)+4=2K+1-k
. Thus, for all
k=1,\ldots,K
,

T_{kk}=2K+1-k-2\cdot\mathbbm{1}\{2k>K\}.

For
1\leq j<k\leq K
, when
j+k>K
, we have
T_{jk}=M_{j}^{\top}M_{k}=-2

where the only non-zero contributions to this inner-product come from rows

(k-j,j),(j,k-j)\in\mathcal{I}
. When
j+k\leq K
, the non-zero contributions come
from rows
(k-j,j),(j,k-j),(j,k),(k,j)\in\mathcal{I}
, and these cancel
exactly to yield
T_{jk}=M_{j}^{\top}M_{k}=0
. Combining these diagonal and
off-diagonal components,
T
has the form

T=\left(\begin{matrix}2K&~&~&~\\
~&2K-1&~&~\\
~&~&\ddots&~\\
~&~&~&K+1\end{matrix}\right)-\left(\begin{matrix}~&~&~&2\\
~&~&2&2\\
~&\begin{rotate}$\ddots$\end{rotate}&\vdots&\vdots\\
2&\ldots&2&2\end{matrix}\right)

where the second matrix accounts also for the term
-2\cdot\mathbbm{1}\{2k>K\}
of the
diagonal entries.

Now let
\lambda>0
be a positive eigenvalue of
T
, with non-zero eigenvector

x=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{K})
. This must be orthogonal to the null vector

(1,2,\ldots,K)
so
x_{1}+2x_{2}+\ldots+Kx_{K}=0
.
From the above form of
T
, the equation
Tx=\lambda x
may be arranged as the
linear system

\displaystyle(2K-\lambda)x_{1}

\displaystyle=2x_{K}

\displaystyle(2K-1-\lambda)x_{2}

\displaystyle=2(x_{K-1}+x_{K})

\displaystyle(K+2-\lambda)x_{K-1}

\displaystyle=2(x_{2}+\ldots+x_{K})

\displaystyle(K+1-\lambda)x_{K}

\displaystyle=2(x_{1}+x_{2}+\ldots+x_{K}).

Summing these equations and adding
x_{1}+2x_{2}+\ldots+Kx_{K}
to both sides, we
obtain

(2K+1-\lambda)(x_{1}+x_{2}+\ldots+x_{K})=2(x_{1}+2x_{2}+\ldots+Kx_{K})+(x_{1}+2x_{2}+\ldots+Kx_{K})=0.

If
x_{1}+x_{2}+\ldots+x_{K}\neq 0
, then this implies
\lambda=2K+1
.
If
x_{1}+x_{2}+\ldots+x_{K}=0
, but
\lambda\notin\{K+1,K+2,\ldots,2K\}
, then
from the above linear system, we have the implications

\displaystyle(K+1-\lambda)x_{K}=2(x_{1}+x_{2}+\ldots+x_{K})=0

\displaystyle\Rightarrow x_{K}=0

\displaystyle(2K-\lambda)x_{1}=2x_{K}=0

\displaystyle\Rightarrow x_{1}=0

\displaystyle(K+2-\lambda)x_{K-1}=2(x_{2}+x_{3}+\ldots+x_{K})=2(0-x_{1})=0

\displaystyle\Rightarrow x_{K-1}=0

\displaystyle(2K-1-\lambda)x_{2}=2(x_{K-1}+x_{K})=0

\displaystyle\Rightarrow x_{2}=0

and so forth. Then
x_{1}=x_{2}=\ldots=x_{K}=0
, which contradicts
x\neq 0
. Thus
any positive eigenvalue
\lambda
of
T
is one of the values

\{K+1,K+2,\ldots,2K+1\}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.1
,

\hat{\theta}=\hat{\theta}^{\mathrm{oracle}}
.

Recall the loss upper bound from Proposition
3.1
.
For constants
C,C^{\prime}>0
, applying
ab\leq a^{2}+b^{2}
,

\displaystyle L(\hat{\theta}^{\mathrm{oracle}},\theta^{*})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}(\hat{r}_{k}-r_{k})^{2}+C\inf_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\hat{r}_{k}r_{k}\big|\hat{\phi}_{k}^{\mathrm{oracle}}-\phi_{k}+k\alpha\big|_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}(\hat{r}_{k}-r_{k})^{2}+C\inf_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}[r_{k}^{2}+r_{k}(\hat{r}_{k}-r_{k})]\big|\hat{\phi}_{k}^{\mathrm{oracle}}-\phi_{k}+k\alpha\big|_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(C+1)\sum_{k=1}^{K}(\hat{r}_{k}-r_{k})^{2}+C\inf_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}r_{k}^{2}\left(\big|\hat{\phi}_{k}^{\mathrm{oracle}}-\phi_{k}+k\alpha\big|_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}+\big|\hat{\phi}_{k}^{\mathrm{oracle}}-\phi_{k}+k\alpha\big|_{\mathcal{A}}^{4}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{K}(\hat{r}_{k}-r_{k})^{2}+\inf_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}r_{k}^{2}\big|\hat{\phi}_{k}^{\mathrm{oracle}}-\phi_{k}+k\alpha\big|_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}\right).

The expectation may be bounded using Corollaries
4.3

and
4.8
. For the bound (
30
) of
Corollary
4.8
, fixing
c>0
be the constant in the
exponent, observe that the given condition for
N
with
C_{0}>0
large enough
implies

c\left(\frac{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{6}}{\sigma^{6}}\wedge\frac{N^{2/3}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)\geq\frac{c}{2}\left(\frac{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{6}}{\sigma^{6}}\wedge\frac{N^{2/3}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)+3\log K.

We may then apply
e^{-(c/2)x},e^{-(c/2)x^{2/3}}\leq C/x

for a constant
C>0
to obtain

e^{-c(\frac{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{6}}{\sigma^{6}}\wedge\frac{N^{2/3}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}})}\leq\frac{C}{K^{3}}\left(\frac{\sigma^{6}}{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{6}}+\frac{\sigma^{3}}{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{3}}\right)\leq\frac{C^{\prime}}{K^{3}}\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}+\frac{\sigma^{6}}{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{6}}\right).

(73)

Then Corollary
4.8
gives simply

\mathbb{E}\left[\inf_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}r_{k}^{2}|\hat{\phi}_{k}^{\mathrm{oracle}}-\phi_{k}+k\alpha|_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}\right]\leq\frac{C^{\prime}\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{K}\left(\frac{K\sigma^{2}}{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}+\frac{\sigma^{6}}{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{6}}\right)

and combining this with Corollary
4.3
yields the lemma.
∎

A.4
Estimation of
\Phi_{k,l}
by optimization

Proof of Lemma
4.9
.

Set
v=\phi-\phi^{\prime}
. We must show: If
v\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
is such that

|v_{k+l}-v_{k}-v_{l}|_{\mathcal{A}}\leq\delta\text{ for all }(k,l)\in\mathcal{I}

(74)

then there exists
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
with
|v_{k}-k\alpha|_{\mathcal{A}}\leq\delta
for all

k=1,\ldots,K
.

We induct on
K
. For
K=1
the result holds trivially by setting

\alpha=v_{1}
. Suppose the result holds for
K-1
. Consider
v\in\mathbb{R}^{K}

that satisfies
(
74
). By the induction hypothesis, there exists
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}

such that
|v_{k}-k\alpha|_{\mathcal{A}}\leq\delta
for
k=1,\ldots,K-1
. Then by
the triangle inequality, it is immediate to see that

|v_{K}-K\alpha|_{\mathcal{A}}\leq|v_{K}-v_{1}-v_{K-1}|_{\mathcal{A}}+|v_{1}-\alpha|_{\mathcal{A}}+|v_{K-1}-(K-1)\alpha|_{\mathcal{A}}\leq 3\delta.

To complete the induction, we must show the stronger bound of
\delta
instead
of
3\delta
.

For this, let

\alpha_{*}=\argmin_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}\Big(\max_{k=1}^{K-1}|v_{k}-k\alpha|_{\mathcal{A}}\Big),\qquad\varepsilon_{k}=|v_{k}-k\alpha_{*}|_{\mathcal{A}}\text{ for }k=1,\ldots,K-1,\qquad\varepsilon=\max_{k=1}^{K-1}\varepsilon_{k}.

Note that a minimizing
\alpha_{*}
exists because the minimum may equivalently
be restricted to the compact domain
[-\pi,\pi]
.
The induction hypothesis implies
\varepsilon\leq\delta
.
By definition of
|\cdot|_{\mathcal{A}}
, there exists
j_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}

for each
k=1,\ldots,K-1
such that

\varepsilon_{k}=|v_{k}-k\alpha_{*}+2\pi j_{k}|.

Furthermore, we claim that there must exist two indices

k,l\in\{1,\ldots,K-1\}
for which

\varepsilon=v_{k}-k\alpha_{*}+2\pi j_{k}\quad\text{ and }\quad{-}\varepsilon=v_{l}-l\alpha_{*}+2\pi j_{l}.

This is because

\Big\{k\in\{1,\ldots,K-1\}:\varepsilon_{k}=\varepsilon\Big\}=\Big\{k\in\{1,\ldots,K-1\}:|v_{k}-k\alpha_{*}+2\pi j_{k}|=\varepsilon\Big\}

is non-empty by definition of
\varepsilon
. If
v_{k}-k\alpha_{*}+2\pi j_{k}=\varepsilon

for every
k
belonging to this set, then we may
decrease the value of
\max_{k=1}^{K-1}|v_{k}-k\alpha_{*}|_{\mathcal{A}}
by slightly
increasing
\alpha_{*}
, which contradicts the optimality of
\alpha_{*}
.
Similarly if
v_{k}-k\alpha_{*}+2\pi j_{k}=-\varepsilon
for all
k
in this set, then
we may decrease
\max_{k=1}^{K-1}|v_{k}-k\alpha_{*}|_{\mathcal{A}}
by slightly
decreasing
\alpha_{*}
, again contradicting the optimality of
\alpha_{*}
.
Thus the claimed indices
k,l
exist.

Then, for this index
k\in\{1,\ldots,K-1\}
, we have

v_{k}-k\alpha_{*}\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}+\varepsilon,\qquad v_{K-k}-(K-k)\alpha_{*}\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}+[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon],\qquad v_{K}-v_{k}-v_{K-k}\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}+[-\delta,\delta].

Adding these three conditions and applying
\varepsilon\leq\delta
,

v_{K}-K\alpha_{*}\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}+[-\delta,2\varepsilon+\delta]\subseteq 2\pi\mathbb{Z}+[-\delta,3\delta].

Similarly, for this index
l\in\{1,\ldots,K-1\}
, we have

v_{l}-l\alpha_{*}\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}-\varepsilon,\qquad v_{K-l}-(K-l)\alpha_{*}\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}+[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon],\qquad v_{K}-v_{l}-v_{K-l}\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}+[-\delta,\delta].

Then adding these conditions, also

v_{K}-K\alpha_{*}\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}+[-2\varepsilon-\delta,\delta]\subseteq 2\pi\mathbb{Z}+[-3\delta,\delta].

Since
3\delta<\pi
strictly, the above two conditions combine to show that

v_{K}-K\alpha_{*}\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}+[-\delta,\delta]
,
i.e.
|v_{K}-K\alpha_{*}|_{\mathcal{A}}\leq\delta
. This completes the induction.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.1
,

\hat{\theta}=\hat{\theta}^{\mathrm{opt}}
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be the event where (
33
) holds. Note that this
is exactly the event where
|\hat{\Phi}_{k,l}^{\mathrm{oracle}}(\phi)-\Phi_{k,l}|<\pi/12
for all

(k,l)\in\mathcal{I}
. Then by Lemma
4.4
and a union bound,

\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}^{c}]\leq CK^{2}\,e^{-c(\frac{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{6}}{\sigma^{6}}\wedge\frac{N^{2/3}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}})}\leq\frac{C^{\prime}}{K}\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}+\frac{\sigma^{6}}{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{6}}\right)

where the second inequality holds under the given condition for
N

as argued in (
73
). Applying
Corollaries
4.10
and
4.8
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\inf_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}r_{k}^{2}|\hat{\phi}_{k}^{\mathrm{opt}}-\phi_{k}+k\alpha|_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{E}\}\inf_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}r_{k}^{2}|\hat{\phi}_{k}^{\mathrm{opt}}-\phi_{k}+k\alpha|_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{E}^{c}\}\inf_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}r_{k}^{2}|\hat{\phi}_{k}^{\mathrm{opt}}-\phi_{k}+k\alpha|_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\inf_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}r_{k}^{2}|\hat{\phi}_{k}^{\mathrm{oracle}}(\phi^{\prime})-\phi_{k}^{\prime}+k\alpha|_{\mathcal{A}}^{2}\right]+C\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}\cdot\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}^{c}]\leq\frac{C^{\prime}\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{K}\left(\frac{K\sigma^{2}}{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}+\frac{\sigma^{6}}{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{6}}\right).

This is (up to a universal constant) the same risk bound as established for
the oracle estimator itself in Corollary
4.8
. The
remainder of the proof is then the same as that of Theorem
4.1

for
\hat{\theta}=\hat{\theta}^{\mathrm{oracle}}
.
∎

A.5
Estimation of
\Phi_{k,l}
by frequency
marching

We describe in this section
an alternative frequency marching method for mimicking the oracle
estimator, which is more explicit and computationally efficient but requires a
larger sample size
N
to succeed. Let

\tilde{\phi}_{1}=0

and, for each
k=2,\ldots,K
, set

\tilde{\phi}_{k}=\operatorname{Arg}\hat{B}_{1,k-1}+\tilde{\phi}_{k-1}\bmod 2\pi.

This defines a vector
\tilde{\phi}\in[-\pi,\pi)^{K}
, which we use in place of
(
31
). Then, as in Section
4.2
, define

\tilde{\Phi}_{k,l}=\tilde{\phi}_{k+l}-\tilde{\phi}_{k}-\tilde{\phi}_{l}
with arithmetic
carried out over
\mathbb{R}
, and choose

\hat{\Phi}_{k,l}^{\mathrm{fm}}\in[\tilde{\Phi}_{k,l}-\pi,\tilde{\Phi}_{k,l}+\pi)
as the
unique version of the phase of
\hat{B}_{k,l}
belonging to this range. Finally,
let
\hat{\phi}^{\mathrm{fm}}
be the resulting least-squares estimate of
\phi
in
(
18
), and let
\hat{\theta}^{\mathrm{fm}}
be the resulting estimate of

\theta
. Again, this procedure uses the frequency-marching estimate

\tilde{\phi}
only as a pilot estimate to resolve the phase ambiguity of the
estimated bispectrum, which is then inverted using a least-squares approach.

The following lemma is analogous to Corollary
4.10
, but
requires an improvement for the error of
\operatorname{Arg}\hat{B}_{k,l}
by a factor of

1/K
.

Lemma A.2
.

Suppose

|\operatorname{Arg}\hat{B}_{k,l}-(\phi_{k+l}-\phi_{k}-\phi_{l})|_{\mathcal{A}}<\pi/(6K)\text{ for
every }(k,l)\in\mathcal{I}.

(75)

Then there exists
\phi^{\prime}
equivalent to
\phi
such that

\hat{\Phi}^{\mathrm{fm}}=\hat{\Phi}^{\mathrm{oracle}}(\phi^{\prime})
.

Proof.

For each
k=2,\ldots,K
, by the definition of
\tilde{\phi}_{k}
and
the triangle inequality,

\big|\tilde{\phi}_{k}-\phi_{k}+k\phi_{1}\big|_{\mathcal{A}}\leq\big|\operatorname{Arg}\hat{B}_{1,k-1}-(\phi_{k}-\phi_{1}-\phi_{k-1})\big|_{\mathcal{A}}+\big|\tilde{\phi}_{k-1}-\phi_{k-1}+(k-1)\phi_{1}\big|_{\mathcal{A}}.

Under the given condition, recursively applying this bound and using

\tilde{\phi}_{1}-\phi_{1}+\phi_{1}=0
for
k=1
,

\big|\tilde{\phi}_{k}-\phi_{k}+k\alpha\big|_{\mathcal{A}}\leq\frac{\pi(k-1)}{6K}<\frac{\pi}{6}\text{ for }\alpha=\phi_{1}\text{ and all }k=1,\ldots,K.

This means there exists
\phi^{\prime}
equivalent to
\phi
for which

|\tilde{\phi}_{k}-\phi_{k}^{\prime}|<\pi/6
for all

k=1,\ldots,K
, and the remainder of the argument is the same as in Corollary

4.10
.
∎

The following guarantee is then analogous to Theorem
4.1
,
now describing the estimator
\hat{\theta}^{\mathrm{fm}}
under a requirement for
N
that
is larger by a factor of
K^{2}
.

Proposition A.3
.

Suppose
r_{k}\geq\underaccent{\bar}{r}
for each
k=1,\ldots,K
.
There exist universal constants
C,C_{0}>0
such that if

N\geq C_{0}(\frac{K^{2}\sigma^{6}}{\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{6}}\log K+\frac{K\sigma^{3}}{\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{3}}(\log K)^{3/2})
, then the guarantee (
19
) holds also
for
\hat{\theta}^{\mathrm{fm}}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be the event where (
75
) holds. This is exactly the
event where

|\hat{\Phi}_{k,l}^{\mathrm{oracle}}(\phi)-\Phi_{k,l}|<\pi/(6K)
for all
(k,l)\in\mathcal{I}
. Then by Lemma
4.4
and a union bound,

\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}^{c}]\leq CK^{2}\,e^{-c(\frac{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}{K^{2}\sigma^{2}}\wedge\frac{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{6}}{K^{2}\sigma^{6}}\wedge\frac{N^{2/3}\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}{K^{2/3}\sigma^{2}})}.

Under the given condition for
N
, an argument similar to (
73
)
shows that this implies

\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}^{c}]\leq\frac{C^{\prime}}{K}\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{2}}+\frac{\sigma^{6}}{N\underaccent{\bar}{r}^{6}}\right),

and the remainder of the proof is the same as that of Theorem
4.1
.
∎

Appendix B
Proofs for maximum likelihood estimation in low
noise

B.1
KL divergence and tail bound for the MLE

The following lemma bounds the Gaussian process
\langle\varepsilon,\,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle
which appears in (
42
).

Lemma B.1
.

Let
\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{2K})
. For a universal constant
C>0
,
any
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{2K}
, and any
s,t>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\sup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}|\langle\varepsilon,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle|>t\text{ and }\|\varepsilon\|\leq s\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8\pi\|\theta\|Ks}{t}\cdot e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{8\|\theta\|^{2}}}

(76)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}|\langle\varepsilon,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle|\right]

\displaystyle\leq C\|\theta\|\sqrt{\log K}.

(77)

Proof.

For each fixed
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
, we have
\langle\varepsilon,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\|\theta\|^{2})
. Thus by a
Gaussian tail bound,

\mathbb{P}[|\langle\varepsilon,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle|>t/2]\leq 2e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{8\|\theta\|^{2}}}.

We set
\delta=t/(2\|\theta\|Ks)
and
take
N_{\delta}\subset\mathcal{A}
as a
\delta
-net of
\mathcal{A}=[-\pi,\pi)
in the
metric
|\cdot|_{\mathcal{A}}
, having cardinality

|N_{\delta}|=\frac{2\pi}{\delta}=\frac{4\pi\|\theta\|Ks}{t}.

For any
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
such that
|\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}|_{\mathcal{A}}\leq\delta
,
from the definition (
3
) of the diagonal blocks of

g(\alpha)
, we have

\|g(\alpha)-g(\alpha^{\prime})\|_{\text{op}}\leq K\delta
. Thus, on the event

\{\|\varepsilon\|\leq s\}
,

\big|\langle\varepsilon,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle-\langle\varepsilon,g(\alpha^{\prime})\cdot\theta\rangle\big|\leq K\delta s\|\theta\|=t/2.

So

\mathbb{P}\left[\sup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}|\langle\varepsilon,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle|>t\text{ and }\|\varepsilon\|\leq s\right]\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\sup_{\alpha\in N_{\delta}}|\langle\varepsilon,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle|>t/2\right]\leq|N_{\delta}|\cdot 2e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{8\|\theta\|^{2}}}

which yields (
76
). Applying (
76
) with
s=\sqrt{4K}

and integrating from
t=4\|\theta\|\sqrt{\log K}
to
t=\infty
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}|\langle\varepsilon,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle|\cdot\mathbf{1}\{\|\varepsilon\|\leq\sqrt{4K}\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[4\|\theta\|\sqrt{\log K}+\int_{4\|\theta\|\sqrt{\log K}}^{\infty}\mathbf{1}\left\{\sup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}|\langle\varepsilon,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle|>t\text{ and }\|\varepsilon\|\leq\sqrt{4K}\right\}dt\right]

\displaystyle\leq 4\|\theta\|\sqrt{\log K}+\int_{4\|\theta\|\sqrt{\log K}}^{\infty}\frac{16\pi\|\theta\|K^{3/2}}{t}e^{-t^{2}/8\|\theta\|^{2}}dt

\displaystyle=4\|\theta\|\sqrt{\log K}+16\pi\|\theta\|K^{3/2}\int_{4\sqrt{\log K}}^{\infty}e^{-t^{2}/8}dt\leq C\|\theta\|\sqrt{\log K}

for a universal constant
C>0
and any
K\geq 2
. Applying a chi-squared tail
bound, we have also

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}|\langle\varepsilon,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle|\cdot\mathbf{1}\{\|\varepsilon\|\geq\sqrt{4K}\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\|\theta\|\cdot\mathbb{E}\Big[\|\varepsilon\|\cdot\mathbf{1}\{\|\varepsilon\|\geq\sqrt{4K}\}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\|\theta\|\cdot\mathbb{E}[\|\varepsilon\|^{2}]^{1/2}\mathbb{P}[\|\varepsilon\|\geq\sqrt{4K}]^{1/2}\leq\|\theta\|\cdot\sqrt{2K}\cdot e^{-cK}

for a universal constant
c>0
.
Combining the above gives (
77
).
∎

The next lemma formalizes the statement (
51
) obtained by a
Taylor expansion around
\alpha=0
.

Lemma B.2
.

Suppose Assumption
5.1
holds.
Fix any constant
\delta_{0}\in[0,3c_{\mathrm{gen}}/8]
.
Then there are constants
C,c>0
depending only on
c_{\mathrm{gen}}
(and
independent of
\delta_{0}
) such that for all

\alpha\in[-\frac{\delta_{0}}{K},\frac{\delta_{0}}{K}]
,

cK^{2}\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}\alpha^{2}\leq\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}-\langle\theta^{*},g(\alpha)\cdot\theta^{*}\rangle\leq CK^{2}\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}\alpha^{2}.

(78)

Furthermore, there is a constant
\iota>0

depending only on
c_{\mathrm{gen}},\delta_{0}
such that for all

\alpha\in[-\pi,\pi)\setminus[-\frac{\delta_{0}}{K},\frac{\delta_{0}}{K}]
,

\langle\theta^{*},g(\alpha)\cdot\theta^{*}\rangle\leq(1-\iota)\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}.

(79)

Proof.

We write as shorthand

r_{k}=r_{k}(\theta^{*})
. From (
4
), observe that

\langle\theta^{*},\,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta^{*}\rangle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}r_{k}^{2}\cos k\alpha=\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}-\sum_{k=1}^{K}r_{k}^{2}(1-\cos k\alpha).

(80)

This is an even function of
\alpha
, so it suffices
to consider
\alpha\in[0,\pi]
.
Suppose first that
0\leq\alpha\leq\delta_{0}/K
. By Taylor expansion
around
\alpha=0
,

\sum_{k=1}^{K}r_{k}^{2}(1-\cos k\alpha)=\sum_{k=1}^{K}r_{k}^{2}\cdot\frac{k^{2}\alpha^{2}}{2}+r(\alpha),

where
|r(\alpha)|\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}r_{k}^{2}(k\alpha)^{3}/6
. Observe that

\sum_{k=1}^{K}k^{3}r_{k}^{2}\leq K^{3}\|\theta^{*}\|^{2},\qquad\sum_{k=1}^{K}k^{2}r_{k}^{2}\leq K^{2}\|\theta^{*}\|^{2},\qquad\sum_{k=1}^{K}k^{2}r_{k}^{2}\geq(K/2)^{2}\sum_{k=\lceil K/2\rceil}^{K}r_{k}^{2}\geq\frac{c_{\mathrm{gen}}K^{2}\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{4},

(81)

the last inequality applying Assumption
5.1
.
Then for
0\leq\alpha\leq\frac{\delta_{0}}{K}\leq\frac{3c_{\mathrm{gen}}}{8K}
, applying the first and third
of these bounds, we have
|r(\alpha)|\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}r_{k}^{2}(k\alpha)^{2}/4
. Applying this and the above Taylor expansion
to (
80
) gives

\sum_{k=1}^{K}r_{k}^{2}\cdot\frac{k^{2}\alpha^{2}}{4}\leq\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}-\langle\theta^{*},g(\alpha)\cdot\theta^{*}\rangle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}r_{k}^{2}\cdot\frac{3k^{2}\alpha^{2}}{4},

which implies (
78
) by the second and third bounds of
(
81
).

Now consider
\delta_{0}/K<\alpha\leq\pi
.
In the sequence
(\cos\alpha,\cos 2\alpha,\ldots,\cos K\alpha)
, we claim that
there are at most
\lceil K/2\rceil

items belonging to the interval
[\cos L,1]
, where
L=\min(\delta_{0}/2,\pi/8)
:

•

If
\frac{\delta_{0}}{K}<\alpha<\frac{\pi}{K}
,
then
\alpha,2\alpha,\ldots,K\alpha\in(0,\pi)
. So
\cos(k\alpha)\in[\cos L,1]
implies that
k\alpha\in[0,L]
, and the number of such items is
at most
L/\alpha\leq(\delta_{0}/2)/(\delta_{0}/K)=K/2
.

•

If
\frac{t\pi}{K}\leq\alpha<\frac{(t+1)\pi}{K}
for some
1\leq t\leq\frac{K}{4}-1
, then
\alpha,2\alpha,\ldots,K\alpha\in(0,(t+1)\pi)
. So

\cos(k\alpha)\in[\cos L,1]
implies that
k\alpha
falls into one of

t+1
closed intervals of width
L
, and the number of such items is at most

(t+1)\cdot\left\lceil\frac{L}{\alpha}\right\rceil\leq(t+1)\cdot\left(\frac{\pi/8}{t\pi/K}+1\right)=\frac{K}{8}+\frac{K}{8t}+t+1\leq\frac{K}{8}+\frac{K}{8}+\frac{K}{4}=\frac{K}{2}.

•

If
\frac{\pi}{4}<\alpha\leq\pi
, then any two consecutive items
\cos k\alpha
and
\cos(k+1)\alpha
cannot both belong to
[\cos L,1]
,
since
\alpha>\frac{\pi}{4}\geq 2L
. Therefore, the number of items would not
exceed
\lceil K/2\rceil
.

Denoting
B=\{k:\cos k\alpha\notin[\cos L,1]\}
, we then have

|B|\geq\lfloor K/2\rfloor
and
1-\cos L\geq c
a small constant depending
on
\delta_{0}
, so

\sum_{k=1}^{K}r_{k}^{2}(1-\cos k\alpha)\geq\sum_{k\in B}r_{k}^{2}(1-\cos L)\geq c\cdot c_{\mathrm{gen}}\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}\geq\iota\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}

for a constant
\iota>0
depending only on
c_{\mathrm{gen}},\delta_{0}
.
Applying this to (
80
) gives (
79
).
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.4
.

Recall the form (
50
) for the KL divergence.
For
\mathrm{II}
, upper bounding the average over
\alpha
by the maximum,

\displaystyle\mathrm{II}\leq\mathbb{E}\log\sup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\exp\left(\frac{\langle\theta^{*}+\sigma\varepsilon,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle}{\sigma^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\langle\theta^{*},g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle}{\sigma^{2}}+\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\sup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\langle\varepsilon,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle}{\sigma}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\langle\theta^{*},g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle}{\sigma^{2}}+\frac{C\|\theta\|}{\sigma}\cdot\sqrt{\log K},

(82)

where the last inequality applies (
77
) from Lemma

B.1
. Similarly, to lower bound
\mathrm{I}
, let us set

\delta_{0}=3c_{\mathrm{gen}}/8
and apply

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\log\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\exp\left(\frac{\langle\theta^{*},g(\alpha)\cdot\theta^{*}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)d\alpha\cdot\inf_{\alpha}\exp\left(\frac{\varepsilon,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta^{*}}{\sigma}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\log\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\exp\left(\frac{\langle\theta^{*},g(\alpha)\cdot\theta^{*}\rangle}{\sigma^{2}}\right)d\alpha-\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\sup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\langle\varepsilon,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta^{*}\rangle}{\sigma}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\log\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\delta_{0}/K}^{\delta_{0}/K}\exp\left(\frac{\langle\theta^{*},g(\alpha)\cdot\theta^{*}\rangle}{\sigma^{2}}\right)d\alpha-\frac{C\|\theta^{*}\|}{\sigma}\cdot\sqrt{\log K}

Applying the upper bound of (
78
) from
Lemma
B.2
, we have for a constant
C>0
that

\displaystyle\int_{-\delta_{0}/K}^{\delta_{0}/K}\exp\left(\frac{\langle\theta^{*},g(\alpha)\cdot\theta^{*}\rangle}{\sigma^{2}}\right)d\alpha

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left(\frac{\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)\int_{-\delta_{0}/K}^{\delta_{0}/K}\exp\left(-\frac{CK^{2}\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\alpha^{2}\right)d\alpha

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)\left(\frac{2CK^{2}\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{-1/2}\cdot\sqrt{2\pi}\left(1-2\widetilde{\Phi}\left(\sqrt{\frac{2CK^{2}\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}}\cdot\frac{\delta_{0}}{K}\right)\right)

where
\widetilde{\Phi}(x)=\int_{x}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-t^{2}/2}dt
is
the right tail probability of the standard Gaussian law. Applying the given
condition
\sigma^{2}\leq\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}
, the input to
\widetilde{\Phi}
is bounded
below by a positive constant. Then the value for
\widetilde{\Phi}
is bounded
away from
1/2
, so for a constant
C_{2}>0
,

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\delta_{0}/K}^{\delta_{0}/K}\exp\left(\frac{\langle\theta^{*},g(\alpha)\cdot\theta^{*}\rangle}{\sigma^{2}}\right)d\alpha\geq\exp\left(\frac{\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{C_{2}K^{2}\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{-1/2}.

(83)

Thus

\mathrm{I}\geq\frac{\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{C_{2}K^{2}\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)-\frac{C\|\theta^{*}\|}{\sigma}\cdot\sqrt{\log K}.

(84)

Combining (
50
), (
82
), and
(
84
) and applying

\min_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\|\theta^{*}-g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\|^{2}=\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}+\|\theta\|^{2}-2\sup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\langle\theta^{*},g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle

yields the lemma.
∎

The following lemma establishes concentration of
R_{N}(\theta)
around
its mean
R(\theta)
, uniformly over bounded domains of
\theta
.

Lemma B.3
.

For a universal constant
c>0
, any
M>0
, and any
t>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\sup_{\theta:\|\theta\|\leq M}|R_{N}(\theta)-R(\theta)|>4t\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt\leq 2\left(1+\tfrac{2M\sqrt{2\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}+(4K+4t)\sigma^{2}}}{t\sigma^{2}}\right)^{2K}e^{-\frac{cN\sigma^{2}t^{2}}{M^{2}}}+4e^{-cN(t\wedge\frac{t^{2}}{K}\wedge\frac{t^{2}\sigma^{2}}{\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}})}.

(85)

Proof.

Recalling the form of
R_{N}(\theta)
from (
42
), we have

R_{N}(\theta)=\mathrm{I}-\mathrm{II}(\theta)+\operatorname{const}(\theta)

where

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\frac{\|\theta^{*}+\sigma\varepsilon^{(m)}\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}},

\displaystyle\mathrm{II}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}f(\varepsilon^{(m)},\theta):=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\log\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\exp\left(\frac{\langle\theta^{*}+\sigma\varepsilon^{(m)},g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle}{\sigma^{2}}\right)d\alpha,

and
\operatorname{const}(\theta)
is a term not depending on the randomness

\{\varepsilon^{(m)}\}
. We analyze separately the concentration of the terms

\mathrm{I}
and
\mathrm{II}(\theta)
.

For the given value
t>0
, define the event

\mathcal{E}=\{|\mathrm{I}-\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{I}]|<2t\}
. We have

\frac{\|\theta^{*}+\sigma\varepsilon^{(m)}\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}=\frac{\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}+\frac{\langle\theta^{*},\varepsilon^{(m)}\rangle}{\sigma}+\frac{\|\varepsilon^{(m)}\|^{2}}{2}.

Here the first term is deterministic, and the latter two terms satisfy

N^{-1}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\langle\theta^{*},\varepsilon^{(m)}\rangle\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}/N)
and
\sum_{m=1}^{N}\|\varepsilon^{(m)}\|^{2}\sim\chi_{2KN}^{2}
. Then by standard Gaussian and chi-squared tail bounds, for
a universal constant
c>0
and any
t>0
,

\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\frac{\langle\theta^{*},\varepsilon^{(m)}\rangle}{\sigma}\right|\geq t\right]\leq 2e^{-\frac{Nt^{2}\sigma^{2}}{2\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}},\qquad\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\frac{\|\varepsilon^{(m)}\|^{2}}{2}-K\right|\geq t\right]\leq 2e^{-cNK(\frac{t}{K}\wedge\frac{t^{2}}{K^{2}})}.

So

\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}^{c}]=\mathbb{P}\Big[|\mathrm{I}-\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{I}]|\geq 2t\Big]\leq 2e^{-\frac{Nt^{2}\sigma^{2}}{2\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}}+2e^{-cNK(\frac{t}{K}\wedge\frac{t^{2}}{K^{2}})}\leq 4e^{-c^{\prime}N(t\wedge\frac{t^{2}}{K}\wedge\frac{t^{2}\sigma^{2}}{\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}})}.

(86)

On the event
\mathcal{E}
, we have

|R_{N}(\theta)-R(\theta)|\leq 2t+|\mathrm{II}(\theta)-\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{II}(\theta)]|
as well as

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\|\theta^{*}+\sigma\varepsilon^{(m)}\|^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}[\|\theta^{*}+\sigma\varepsilon^{(m)}\|^{2}]+4t\sigma^{2}=\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}+(2K+4t)\sigma^{2}.

(87)

Recalling the probability law
\mathcal{P}_{\theta,\varepsilon}
from
(
43
), the
\varepsilon
-gradient of the function

f(\varepsilon,\theta)
defining
\mathrm{II}(\theta)
is bounded as

\|\nabla_{\varepsilon}f(\varepsilon,\theta)\|=\left\|\frac{1}{\sigma}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha\sim\mathcal{P}_{\theta,\varepsilon}}\left[g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\right]\right\|\leq\frac{1}{\sigma}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha\sim\mathcal{P}_{\theta,\varepsilon}}\left[\|g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\|\right]=\frac{\|\theta\|}{\sigma}.

Thus
f(\varepsilon,\theta)
is
\frac{\|\theta\|}{\sigma}
-Lipschitz
in
\varepsilon
. Then by Gaussian concentration of measure,
for universal constants
C,c>0
, we have that

f(\varepsilon,\theta)-\mathbb{E}[f(\varepsilon,\theta)]

is
\frac{C\|\theta\|}{\sigma}
-subgaussian, and Hoeffding’s inequality yields

\mathbb{P}\left[|\mathrm{II}(\theta)-\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{II}(\theta)]|>t\right]=\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}f(\varepsilon^{(m)},\theta)-\mathbb{E}[f(\varepsilon^{(m)},\theta)]\right|>t\right]\leq 2e^{-\frac{cN\sigma^{2}t^{2}}{\|\theta\|^{2}}}.

Now to obtain uniform concentration over
\{\theta:\|\theta\|\leq M\}
,
set
\delta=t\sigma^{2}/\sqrt{2\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}+(4K+4t)\sigma^{2}}
,
and let
N_{\delta}
be a
\delta
-net of

\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{2K}:\|\theta\|\leq M\}
having cardinality

|N_{\delta}|\leq\left(1+\frac{2M}{\delta}\right)^{2K}=\left(1+\frac{2M\sqrt{2\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}+(4K+4t)\sigma^{2}}}{t\sigma^{2}}\right)^{2K}.

The
\theta
-gradient
of
\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{II}(\theta)]=\mathbb{E}[f(\varepsilon^{(m)},\theta)]
is bounded as

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\theta}\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{II}(\theta)]\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\alpha\sim\mathcal{P}_{\theta,\varepsilon}}[g(\alpha)^{-1}(\theta^{*}+\sigma\varepsilon)]\right]\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\mathbb{E}[\|\theta^{*}+\sigma\varepsilon\|]\leq\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[\|\theta^{*}+\sigma\varepsilon\|^{2}]}=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\sqrt{\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}+2K\sigma^{2}}.

Similarly, on the event
\mathcal{E}
, the
\theta
-gradient of
\mathrm{II}(\theta)

without expectation is bounded as

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\theta}\mathrm{II}(\theta)\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha\sim\mathcal{P}_{\theta,\varepsilon^{(m)}}}[g(\alpha)^{-1}(\theta^{*}+\sigma\varepsilon^{(m)})]\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\|\theta^{*}+\sigma\varepsilon^{(m)}\|\leq\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\sqrt{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\|\theta^{*}+\sigma\varepsilon^{(m)}\|^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\sqrt{\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}+(2K+4t)\sigma^{2}},

the last inequality applying (
87
). Therefore

\mathrm{II}(\theta)-\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{II}(\theta)]
is Lipschitz in
\theta
, with
Lipschitz constant at most

\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\sqrt{\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}+2K\sigma^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\sqrt{\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}+(2K+4t)\sigma^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\sqrt{2\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}+(4K+4t)\sigma^{2}}=\frac{t}{\delta}.

Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\sup_{\theta:\|\theta\|\leq M}|R_{N}(\theta)-R(\theta)|>4t\text{ and }\mathcal{E}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\sup_{\theta:\|\theta\|\leq M}|\mathrm{II}(\theta)-\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{II}(\theta)]|>2t\text{ and }\mathcal{E}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\sup_{\theta\in N_{\delta}}|\mathrm{II}(\theta)-\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{II}(\theta)]|>t\right]\leq 2|N_{\delta}|e^{-\frac{cN\sigma^{2}t^{2}}{M^{2}}}.

Combining this with (
86
) gives (
85
).
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.3
.

For the given value of
\delta_{1}
and each integer
n\geq 1
, define

\Gamma_{n}=\left\{\theta:n\delta_{1}\|\theta^{*}\|\leq\min_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\|\theta^{*}-g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\|<(n+1)\delta_{1}\|\theta^{*}\|\right\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2K}.

Observe that
\|\theta\|\leq[1+(n+1)\delta_{1}]\|\theta^{*}\|
for
\theta\in\Gamma_{n}
, so Lemma
5.4
implies

D_{\mathrm{KL}}(p_{\theta^{*}}\|p_{\theta})\geq\frac{n^{2}\delta_{1}^{2}\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{C_{2}K^{2}\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)-\frac{[2+(n+1)\delta_{1}]C_{3}\|\theta^{*}\|}{\sigma}\sqrt{\log K}\text{ for all }\theta\in\Gamma_{n}.

Then, under the
given assumption that
\frac{\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\geq C_{1}\log K
for a
sufficiently large constant
C_{1}>0
(depending on
c_{\mathrm{gen}},\delta_{1}
),
setting

t_{n}=c_{0}n^{2}\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}/\sigma^{2}

for a sufficiently small constant
c_{0}>0
, the above implies that

D_{\mathrm{KL}}(p_{\theta^{*}}\|p_{\theta})\geq 10t_{n}\text{ for all }\theta\in\Gamma_{n}.

Applying (
85
) with
t=t_{n}

and
M=[1+(n+1)\delta_{1}]\|\theta^{*}\|
gives,
for some constants

C,C^{\prime},c,c^{\prime}>0
depending on
\delta_{1}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\sup_{\theta:\|\theta\|\leq[1+(n+1)\delta_{1}]\|\theta^{*}\|}|R_{N}(\theta)-R(\theta)|>4t_{n}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(1+C\sqrt{1+\tfrac{K\sigma^{2}}{n^{2}\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}}\right)^{2K}e^{-cn^{2}N\cdot\frac{\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}}+4e^{-cn^{2}N\cdot\frac{\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}(1\wedge\frac{n^{2}\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{K\sigma^{2}})}

\displaystyle\leq(C^{\prime}K)^{K}e^{-c^{\prime}nN\log K}+e^{-c^{\prime}nN(\log K)^{2}/K}

where the last line applies
n\geq 1
and
\frac{\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\geq C_{1}\log K
to simplify the bound.
On the event where
|R_{N}(\theta)-R(\theta)|\leq 4t_{n}
and

|R_{N}(\theta^{*})-R(\theta^{*})|\leq 4t_{n}
, since

D_{\mathrm{KL}}(p_{\theta^{*}}\|p_{\theta})=R(\theta)-R(\theta^{*})\geq 10t_{n}
,
we must then have

R_{N}(\theta)-R_{N}(\theta^{*})\geq 2t_{n}>0
so that
\theta
is not the MLE. Thus,

\mathbb{P}\left[\hat{\theta}^{\text{MLE}}\in\Gamma_{n}\right]\leq(C^{\prime}K)^{K}e^{-c^{\prime}nN\log K}+e^{-c^{\prime}nN(\log K)^{2}/K}.

(88)

Summing over all
n\geq 1
and recalling our choice of rotation for

\hat{\theta}^{\text{MLE}}
that satisfies (
36
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\|\hat{\theta}^{\text{MLE}}-\theta^{*}\|\geq\delta_{1}\|\theta^{*}\|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\hat{\theta}^{\text{MLE}}\in\Gamma_{n}\right]\leq(C^{\prime}K)^{K}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}e^{-c^{\prime}nN\log K}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}e^{-c^{\prime}nN(\log K)^{2}/K}.

Under the given assumption
N\geq C_{0}K

for a sufficiently large constant
C_{0}>0
, both exponents
c^{\prime}N\log K
and

c^{\prime}N(\log K)^{2}/K
are bounded below by a constant. Then summing these
geometric series gives, for some modified constants
C,c,c^{\prime}>0
,

\mathbb{P}\left[\|\hat{\theta}^{\text{MLE}}-\theta^{*}\|\geq\delta_{1}\|\theta^{*}\|\right]\leq(CK)^{K}e^{-cN\log K}+e^{-cN(\log K)^{2}/K}\leq e^{-c^{\prime}N(\log K)^{2}/K}

where the second inequality holds again under the assumption
N\geq C_{0}K
.
This shows (
47
).

To show (
48
), we apply
\|\theta\|\leq[1+(n+1)\delta_{1}]\|\theta^{*}\|
for
\theta\in\Gamma_{n}
and
(
88
) to get, for some constants
C,C^{\prime},c^{\prime}>0
depending on

\delta_{1}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\hat{\theta}^{\text{MLE}}\|^{4}]

\displaystyle\leq[(1+\delta_{1})\|\theta^{*}\|]^{4}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}[(1+(n+1)\delta_{1})\|\theta^{*}\|]^{4}\cdot\mathbb{P}[\hat{\theta}^{\text{MLE}}\in\Gamma_{n}]

\displaystyle\leq C\|\theta^{*}\|^{4}\left(1+(C^{\prime}K)^{K}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n^{4}e^{-c^{\prime}nN\log K}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n^{4}e^{-c^{\prime}nN(\log K)^{2}/K}\right).

For a sufficiently large constant
A>0
, we have
n^{4}e^{-An}<e^{-An/2}
for
all
n\geq 1
. Hence, under the condition
N\geq C_{0}K
for sufficiently
large
C_{0}>0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\hat{\theta}^{\text{MLE}}\|^{4}]

\displaystyle\leq C\|\theta^{*}\|^{4}\left(1+(C^{\prime}K)^{K}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}e^{-(c^{\prime}/2)nN\log K}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}e^{-(c^{\prime}/2)nN(\log K)^{2}/K}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\|\theta^{*}\|^{4}\left(1+(C^{\prime}K)^{K}e^{-c^{\prime\prime}N\log K}+e^{-c^{\prime\prime}N(\log K)^{2}/K}\right)\leq C^{\prime}\|\theta^{*}\|^{4}.

∎

B.2
Lower bound for the information matrix

Define the domain

\mathcal{F}_{1}(\theta,\delta_{1})=\left\{\varepsilon:\sup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}|\langle\varepsilon,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle|\leq\frac{\delta_{1}\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{\sigma}\right\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2K}.

(89)

The following deterministic lemma guarantees that the law

\mathcal{P}_{\theta,\varepsilon}
concentrates near 0 when

\varepsilon\in\mathcal{F}_{1}(\theta,\delta_{1})
.

Lemma B.4
.

Suppose Assumption
5.1
holds.
Fix any
\delta_{0}>0
. Then there exist constants
C_{1},\delta_{1}>0
depending
only on
c_{\mathrm{gen}},\delta_{0}
such that if

\sigma^{2}\leq\frac{\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{C_{1}\log K}
, then the following holds:
For any
\theta\in\mathcal{B}(\delta_{1})
and any (deterministic)

\varepsilon\in\mathcal{F}_{1}(\theta,\delta_{1})
,

\sup_{\alpha\in[-\frac{\delta_{0}}{K},\frac{\delta_{0}}{K}]}\langle\theta^{*}+\sigma\varepsilon,\,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle>\sup_{\alpha\in[-\pi,\pi)\setminus[-\frac{\delta_{0}}{K},\frac{\delta_{0}}{K}]}\langle\theta^{*}+\sigma\varepsilon,\,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle.

(90)

Furthermore, for a constant
c>0
depending only on
\underaccent{\bar}{c},\bar{c},\delta_{0}
,

\mathbb{P}_{\alpha\sim\mathcal{P}_{\theta,\varepsilon}}\Big[|\alpha|_{\mathcal{A}}>\delta_{0}/K\Big]\leq e^{-c\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}/\sigma^{2}}.

(91)

Proof.

Define
I_{1}=[-\frac{\delta_{0}}{K},\frac{\delta_{0}}{K}]
and

I_{2}=[-\pi,\pi)\setminus[-\frac{\delta_{0}}{K},\frac{\delta_{0}}{K}]
.
Let us write

\langle\theta^{*}+\sigma\varepsilon,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle=\langle\theta^{*},g(\alpha)\theta^{*}\rangle+\langle\theta^{*},g(\alpha)(\theta-\theta^{*})\rangle+\sigma\langle\varepsilon,g(\alpha)\theta\rangle.

The conditions
\theta\in\mathcal{B}(\delta_{1})
and

\varepsilon\in\mathcal{F}_{1}(\theta,\delta_{1})
show for the second and third terms

|\langle\theta^{*},g(\alpha)(\theta-\theta^{*})\rangle|\leq\|\theta^{*}\|\cdot\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|\leq\delta_{1}\|\theta^{*}\|^{2},\qquad|\sigma\langle\varepsilon,g(\alpha)\theta\rangle|\leq\delta_{1}\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}.

(92)

For the first term, Lemma
B.2
implies that for constants

C>0
and
\iota>0
,

\langle\theta^{*},g(\alpha)\theta^{*}\rangle\leq(1-\iota)\cdot\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}\text{ if }\alpha\in I_{2},\qquad\langle\theta^{*},g(\alpha)\theta^{*}\rangle\geq\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}-CK^{2}\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}\alpha^{2}\text{ if }\alpha\in I_{1}.

(93)

Then for all
\alpha\in I_{2}
, we have

\langle\theta^{*}+\sigma\varepsilon,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle\leq(1-\iota+2\delta_{1})\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}
, while for

\alpha=0\in I_{1}
, we have

\langle\theta^{*}+\sigma\varepsilon,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle\geq(1-2\delta_{1})\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}
. Setting
\delta_{1}<\iota/4
, this shows
(
90
).

To show (
91
), we may correspondingly write
the density (
43
) for
the distribution
\mathcal{P}_{\theta,\varepsilon}
as

\frac{d\mathcal{P}_{\theta,\varepsilon}(\alpha)}{d\alpha}\propto\exp\left(\frac{\langle\theta^{*}+\sigma\varepsilon,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle}{\sigma^{2}}\right)=\exp\left(\frac{\langle\theta^{*},g(\alpha)\theta^{*}\rangle}{\sigma^{2}}+\frac{\langle\theta^{*},g(\alpha)(\theta-\theta^{*})\rangle}{\sigma^{2}}+\frac{\sigma\langle\varepsilon,g(\alpha)\theta\rangle}{\sigma^{2}}\right).

Then

\displaystyle\int_{I_{2}}\exp\left(\frac{\langle\theta^{*}+\sigma\varepsilon,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle}{\sigma^{2}}\right)d\alpha

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(\frac{(1-\iota+2\delta_{1})\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right),

\displaystyle\int_{I_{1}}\exp\left(\frac{\langle\theta^{*}+\sigma\varepsilon,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle}{\sigma^{2}}\right)d\alpha

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left(\frac{(1-2\delta_{1})\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)\cdot\int_{-\delta_{0}/K}^{\delta_{0}/K}\exp\left(-\frac{CK^{2}\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}\alpha^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)d\alpha.

Lower bounding this Gaussian integral using the same argument as
(
83
), for a constant
C^{\prime}>0
,

\int_{-\delta_{0}/K}^{\delta_{0}/K}\exp\left(-\frac{CK^{2}\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}\alpha^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)d\alpha\geq\left(\frac{C^{\prime}K^{2}\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{-1/2}\sqrt{2\pi}.

Combining these bounds and choosing
\delta_{1}<\iota/4
,
for a constant
c>0
,

J:=\frac{\int_{I_{1}}\exp\left(\frac{\langle\theta^{*}+\sigma\varepsilon,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle}{\sigma^{2}}\right)d\alpha}{\int_{I_{2}}\exp\left(\frac{\langle\theta^{*}+\sigma\varepsilon,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle}{\sigma^{2}}\right)d\alpha}\geq\left(\frac{C^{\prime}K^{2}\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)^{-1/2}\sqrt{2\pi}\exp\left(\frac{c\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)\geq\exp\left(\frac{c\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)

where the last inequality holds for

\frac{\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\geq C_{1}\log K
and a sufficiently large
constant
C_{1}>0
.
Since
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha\sim\mathcal{P}_{\theta,\varepsilon}}[|\alpha|_{\mathcal{A}}\geq\frac{\delta_{0}}{K}]=1/(1+J)<1/J
, this shows (
91
).
∎

Next, recall the complex representations

\tilde{\theta}=(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{K})\in\mathbb{C}^{K},\qquad\tilde{v}=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{K})\in\mathbb{C}^{K},\qquad\tilde{\varepsilon}=(\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{K})\in\mathbb{C}^{K}

and define
\mathcal{F}_{2}(\theta,v,\delta_{1})\subset\mathbb{R}^{2K}
as the set of
vectors
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{2K}
that satisfy the following three conditions:

\displaystyle\sup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\Big|\langle\varepsilon,g(\alpha)\cdot v\rangle\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|\theta^{*}\|}{\sigma}

(94)

\displaystyle\sup_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in[-\pi,\pi)}\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\left|\operatorname{Re}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\overline{\varepsilon_{k}}e^{ik\alpha^{\prime}}\Big(e^{ik\alpha}-1-ik\alpha\Big)\theta_{k}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\delta_{1}K^{2}\|\theta^{*}\|^{2}}{\sigma}

(95)

\displaystyle\sup_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in[-\pi,\pi)}\frac{1}{|\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}|}\left|\operatorname{Re}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\overline{\varepsilon_{k}}\Big(e^{ik\alpha}-e^{ik\alpha^{\prime}}\Big)v_{k}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\delta_{1}K\|\theta^{*}\|}{\sigma}

(96)

The domain
\mathcal{E}(\theta,v,\delta_{1})
in Lemma
5.6
is given by

\mathcal{F}_{1}(\theta,\delta_{1})\cap\mathcal{F}_{2}(\theta,v,\delta_{1})
.

Proof of Lemma
5.6
.

Let us denote

y=\theta^{*}+\sigma\varepsilon

The idea will be to approximate
\operatorname{Var}_{\alpha\sim\mathcal{P}_{\theta,\varepsilon}}
by the
variance with respect to a Gaussian law over
\alpha
. We fix a small constant

\delta_{0}>0
to be determined, and take
C_{1}>0
large enough
and
\delta_{1}>0
small enough so that the conclusions of Lemma

B.4
hold. Let

\alpha_{0}=\argmax_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\langle y,\,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle

(97)

(where we may take any maximizer if it is not unique). Then (
90
)
guarantees that
\alpha_{0}\in[-\frac{\delta_{0}}{K},\frac{\delta_{0}}{K}]
.

In the sense of Section
3.2
, denote the complex
representations of
\theta^{*},\varepsilon,y,\theta,v\in\mathbb{R}^{2K}
by

\tilde{\theta}^{*}=(\theta_{1}^{*},\ldots,\theta_{K}^{*})\in\mathbb{C}^{K},\qquad\tilde{\varepsilon}=(\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{K})\in\mathbb{C}^{K},\qquad\tilde{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{K})\in\mathbb{C}^{K},

\tilde{\theta}=(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{K})\in\mathbb{C}^{K},\qquad\tilde{v}=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{K})\in\mathbb{C}^{K}.

Then the complex representation of
g(\alpha)\cdot\theta

is
(e^{ik\alpha}\theta_{k}:k=1,\ldots,K)
.
By the inner-product relation (
13
), we have

\langle y,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle=\operatorname{Re}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\overline{y_{k}}\cdot e^{ik\alpha}\theta_{k}.

(98)

The first-order condition for optimality of
\alpha_{0}
in
(
97
) yields

0=\frac{d}{d\alpha}\langle y,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle\Big|_{\alpha=\alpha_{0}}=\operatorname{Re}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\overline{y_{k}}\cdot ike^{ik\alpha_{0}}\cdot\theta_{k}.

Applying this condition and the decomposition

\displaystyle e^{ik\alpha}=e^{ik\alpha_{0}}\Big[1+ik(\alpha-\alpha_{0})+\Big(e^{ik(\alpha-\alpha_{0})}-1-ik(\alpha-\alpha_{0})\Big)\Big]

to (
98
),
we may write the density function (
43
) for
the distribution
\mathcal{P}_{\theta,\varepsilon}
as

\frac{d\mathcal{P}_{\theta,\varepsilon}(\alpha)}{d\alpha}\propto\exp\left(\frac{\langle y,g(\alpha)\cdot\theta\rangle}{\sigma^{2}}\right)\propto\exp\left(\frac{p(\alpha)}{\sigma^{2}}\right),

where (also dropping constant terms that do not depend on
\alpha
)
